, so that the total trace is zero. In this way, ancillary degrees of freedom give access to traceful vectors, and so to the possibility of witnessing non-Markovianity. A similar argument was also presented in
Bylicka et al. 2017
for the case of quantum dynamics. These results lead to the following:

Theorem 8
.

Given a divisible dynamics
\Phi_{t}
, there always exists an ancilla of finite dimension
d_{A}
on which the dynamics acts trivially (i.e., the total evolution is given by
\Phi_{t}\otimes\mathds{I}_{d_{A}}
) such that one can witness any non-Markovianity in
\Phi_{t}
via revivals in the trace distance between initially prepared states.

For quantum states the minimal dimension of the ancilla is
d_{A}=d+1

Bylicka et al. 2017
. It should be noticed, though, that in order to actually speak about complete positivity one always needs an ancilla of dimension at least
d
(as complete positivity coincides with the positivity of the map
\Phi\otimes\mathds{I}_{d}
). In this way, the trace distance needs one extra dimension more than the minimum in order to witness non-Markovianity. A similar result actually holds also for classical systems. In this case, one only needs to enlarge the state space by one extra dimension. Then, for any vector
\boldsymbol{v}
on the original space, one can always construct a traceless state on this extended space as:

\displaystyle\delta\rho=\begin{cases}\delta\rho_{i}=v_{i}&{\rm if}\;i\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\,;\\
\delta\rho_{i}=-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta\rho_{j}&{\rm if}\;i\equiv d+1\,.\end{cases}

(74)

This state satisfies
\mathrm{Tr}\left[|\delta\dot{\rho}|\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[|\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}|\right]
, which proves the claim. Still, in this case it should be noticed that this construction cannot be carried out by adjoining an ancilla, as in that case the minimum dimension of the state space is
d_{A}=2
. Nonetheless, this example is useful as it shows that two dimensional ancillas are enough, and that in principle it would be sufficient to embed the dynamics
\Phi_{t}
in a space only one dimension larger than the original space in order to witness classical non-Markovianity.

III.2
Retrodiction and Fisher information

In the literature about non-Markovianity, one of the most used expression is
backflow of information
. Still, by looking at the usual quantifiers defined to assess it, one might be surprised to discover that for the most part they are actually distinguishability measures accounting for the statistical difference between states
at time
t
- such as the trace distance and Fisher information, on which we focused in the previous section.
Whereas one could in principle justify the interpretation of non-monotonicity for these quantities as actual backflow of information, here we take a different approach: we prove, in fact, that the contractivity of the Fisher information is in one-to-one correspondence with the monotonic degradation in the ability of an agent to retrodict the
initial state
of the system (Thm.
9
).

Before doing so, we need some formalism. First of all, we present a way to simulate the Fisher scalar product
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}
at time
t
by the scalar product at time
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
at time
t=0
. This is obtained by the following rearranging:

\displaystyle K_{f,\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t}(A),\Phi_{t}(B))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{t}(A)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(B)]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\left[\left(\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}^{\dagger}\;\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\right)\Phi_{t}(B)\right]\right]=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(B)]\right]=K_{f^{\prime},\pi}(A,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(B))\,,

(75)

where in the last line we have implicitly defined the
generalised Petz recovery map

\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
, generalizing the famous recovery introduced by Petz
Ohya and Petz 2004
, which is obtained for
f(x)=f^{\prime}(x)=\sqrt{x}
. In this way, the evolution of any Fisher scalar products can be modelled without the need to actually evolve the state, at the cost of introducing a time dependent vector
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(B)
.

We provide here a list of properties that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
satisfies and we defer the proofs of these facts to
Box 8
at the end of the section:

1.

\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
reduces to the Bayes rule for classical dynamics, i.e., if
\Phi_{t}
is a stochastic map, and
\pi
a diagonal state, for any
f
and
f^{\prime}
one has that:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}(\boldsymbol{\cdot})=\sum_{i,j}\;\frac{(\Phi_{t})_{j,i}\,\pi_{i}}{(\Phi_{t}(\pi))_{j}}\,\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left.\left|{j}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|\,;

(76)

2.

if
\pi
is full-rank,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
is trace preserving;

3.

the operator
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}
is positive, and self-adjoint with respect to
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
, meaning that:

\displaystyle K_{f^{\prime},\pi}(A,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(B))=K_{f^{\prime},\pi}(\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(A),B)\,;

(77)

4.

the state
\pi
, called the
prior state
due to the analogy with Bayes rule in Eq. (
76
), is perfectly retrieved by
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(\pi)=\pi\,;

(78)

5.

If
f^{\prime}(x)\leq f(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then the spectrum of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}
is completely contained in
[0,1]
. Moreover, as it can be seen from Eq. (
78
), 1 is always part of the spectrum;

6.

the transformation associating to a map its generalised Petz recovery, which we denote by
P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t}):=\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
, can be reversed as:

\displaystyle P_{(f,f^{\prime}),\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t}))=\Phi_{t}\,;

(79)

7.

In the case of divisible evolutions
\Phi_{t}=\Phi_{t,s}\circ\Phi_{s}
, one can express
P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t})
in terms of
P_{(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}),\pi}(\Phi_{s})
and
P_{(f^{\prime\prime},f),\Phi_{s}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t,s})
, for any
f^{\prime\prime}
, as:

\displaystyle P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t})=P_{(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}),\pi}(\Phi_{s})\circ P_{(f^{\prime\prime},f),\Phi_{s}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t,s})\,.

(80)

Similarly with what happens for the adjoint, the generalised Petz recovery map composes from left to right.

Figure 3:
Depiction of the content of Thm.
9
: consider a dynamics
\Phi_{t}
which is Markovian till some time
t
. This implies that the Fisher distance between any two points on the state space contracts. If between time
t
and
t+{\rm d}t\,
we observe non-Markovianity through the Fisher distance, i.e., two points in the image of
\Phi_{t}
gets farther away, then retrodicting at time
t+{\rm d}t\,
gives a better result than doing so at time
t
(we use the notation
\delta\hat{\rho}_{t}:=\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho)
).

Thanks to Eq. (
76
), one can consider
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
as some kind of generalisation of Bayes rule to quantum systems. This interpretation can be corroborated in the following context:
consider a state
\pi+\delta\rho
, with
\delta\rho
a small perturbation, and evolve it according to
\Phi_{t}
; at this point,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
is applied to recover as much information about the initial state as possible.
Thanks to the definition in Eq. (
75
), there is a one-to-one correspondence between the Fisher information at time
t
, and the Fisher scalar product at the initial state with the retrodicted vector. In formulae, this means that:

\displaystyle K_{f,\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho),\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho))=K_{f^{\prime},\pi}(\pi+\delta\rho,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho))\;.

(81)

Using the property in Eq. (
78
), we can further simplify the above equality to:

\displaystyle 1+K_{f,\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho),\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho))=1+K_{f^{\prime},\pi}(\delta\rho,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho))\;,

(82)

which makes explicit the appearance of the Fisher information. As the above equality shows, increases/decreases of the Fisher information at time
t
correspond to increases/decreases of the overlap between
\pi+\delta\rho
and its retrodiction, as measured by the Fisher scalar product.

At the level of distinguishability measures, the quality of the retrieval can be quantified by the contrast function:

\displaystyle H_{g(f^{\prime})}(\pi+\delta\rho||\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho))=H_{g}(\pi+\delta\rho||\pi+\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho))=

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\delta\rho-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho))\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[(\delta\rho-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho))]\right]+{\mathcal{O}\left(|\delta\rho|^{3}\right)}\,,

(83)

where we introduced the notation
g(f^{\prime})
to indicate the contrast function that locally expands to
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
(i.e.,
g(f^{\prime})
and
f^{\prime}
are related by Eq. (
13
)), and in the first line we used Eq. (
78
) to apply the operators to
\delta\rho
only. Then, the interpretation of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
as a recovery map might suggest that if
\Phi_{t}
is non-Markovian at time
t
, then the divergence above will decrease at that time: thanks to the backflow of information, the ability of an agent to retrodict the initial state improves. Still, from a mathematical point of view, in principle there is no intuitive reason for this to be the case: in fact, the behaviour of
H_{g}(\rho||\sigma)
is characterised only if the same channel is applied on both states, whereas in the equation above
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}
is applied only on the right. Moreover, as we discuss at the end of the section,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}
might not even be a channel in general, further eroding any mathematical justification to the intuition discussed. Still, the following theorem bridges the gap between the intuitive interpretation of Eq. (
83
) and its actual mathematical form, showing that indeed
H_{g(f^{\prime})}(\pi+\delta\rho||\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho))
decreases for non-Markovian dynamics:

Theorem 9
.

Given a divisible dynamics
\Phi_{t}
and a prior state
\pi
, define the generalised Petz map
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
according to Eq. (
75
). If
f^{\prime}(x)\leq f(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we have the implication:

\displaystyle\exists\,\delta\rho\,,\;\;\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho)]\right]>0\;\;\implies\;\;\;\;\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,H_{g(f^{\prime})}(\pi+\delta\rho||\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho))<0\,.

(84)

Since the Fisher information is monotonically contractive under Markovian dynamics, the condition on the left is only attainable in the non-Markovian regime.

To the best of our knowledge, this result is the first that explicitly considers backflow of information in a single state at time
t=0
: indeed, whereas here we compare the initial condition with our best guess about it, the usual approach to non-Markovianity is to compare the behaviour of two different states at time
t
. There are two messages one should get from Thm.
9
: first, it legitimates the intuitive interpretation of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
as a state retrieval map; second, it directly connects the contractivity properties of the Fisher information at time
t
(which is a distinguishability measure), with the improvement in the ability of an agent to retrodict the initial state of the system.

Proof.

Starting from the expansion in Eq. (
83
), and ignoring corrections of the order
{\mathcal{O}\left(|\delta\rho|^{3}\right)}
, we can reassemble the terms as:

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}H_{g(f^{\prime})}(\pi+\delta\rho||\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\pi+\delta\rho))=\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})(\delta\rho))\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})(\delta\rho))]\right]=

(85)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho\;\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})^{2}(\delta\rho))]\right]=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho\;\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})\circ\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}\right)(\delta\rho))]\right]\,,

(86)

where in the first equality is just a rewriting of Eq. (
83
), then we used the self-adjointness property
3
in Eq. (
77
) to group the superoperator
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})
on the right, and finally we carried out the time derivative.

Now, it should be noticed that thanks to the property
5
the spectrum of
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})
is also contained in
[0,1]
. On the other hand,
-\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}
has positive eigenvalues if and only if the Fisher information corresponding to
f
is contractive, as one can see from the standard rewriting:

\displaystyle-\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\;\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})\right)(A)]\right]=-\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{t}(A)\;\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(A))]\right]\,.

(87)

Then, suppose that the Fisher information expands at some point. This means that there exists at least one negative eigenvalue of
-\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}
, which we denote by
\lambda
. Also, denote by
\delta\tilde{\rho}
the corresponding eigenoperator. Then, the right hand side of Eq. (
84
) reads:

\displaystyle K_{f^{\prime},\pi}\left(\delta\tilde{\rho},(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})\circ\left(-\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}\right)(\delta\tilde{\rho})\right)=\lambda\,K_{f^{\prime},\pi}\left(\delta\tilde{\rho},(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})(\delta\tilde{\rho})\right)<0\,,

(88)

where the last step follows from the fact that
\lambda<0
and
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t})
is a positive operator. This concludes the proof.
∎

As we mentioned,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
might not even be a CP map for general evolutions: for example, by choosing
\Phi=\mathds{I}
, the requirement that
\widetilde{\mathds{I}}_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
is CP is equivalent to constraining both
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}
and
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
to be CP. The conditions that are needed to ensure this are deferred to Sec.
IV.3
, but we can already use Thm.
14
to give the most general expression of a CP Petz recovery
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}\nu^{+}_{f^{\prime}}(t)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}\nu^{-}_{f}(s)\;\mathcal{V}_{\pi}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-it\right)\circ\Phi_{t}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(\pi)}\left(is-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\,.

(89)

where
{\rm d}\nu^{+}_{f^{\prime}}
and
{\rm d}\nu^{-}_{f}
are two symmetric probability distribution on the real line, and we introduced the CP map
\mathcal{V}_{\pi}(z)[A]:=\pi^{z}\,A\,(\pi^{z})^{\dagger}
. This expression is somehow reminiscent of the one found in
Junge et al. 2018
for the universal recovery map. Then, it directly follows from Thm.
9
that:

Corollary 9.1
.

Suppose
f
and
f^{\prime}
are chosen such that
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
and
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}
are both CP. Then, there exists a channel
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
of the form in Eq. (
89
) that perfectly retrieves the prior state (i.e.,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(\pi)=\pi
), reduces to the Bayes’ rule for classical dynamics, and such that Eq. (
84
) is satisfied.

The fact that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
can be made CP means that there exists a physical procedure that an agent can carry out to retrodict the initial state of the system after the noisy operation
\Phi
is applied. Moreover, Corollary
9.1
gives a recipe to exploit non-Markovianity for error correction, showing that the backflow of information is indeed beneficial.

Box 8. Proofs of the properties of the generalised Petz recovery
\rightarrow

We present here the derivations of the properties that generalised Petz recovery maps satisfy.

First, regarding condition
1
, i.e., the fact that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
reduces to Bayes rule for classical dynamics this can be seen as follows: a channel implementing a classical evolution takes the form
\Phi_{t}(\cdot)=\sum_{i,j}\,(\Phi_{t})_{i,j}\,\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left.\left|{j}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|
, where
\{\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\}
are part of an orthonormal basis. Moreover, if we restrict to diagonal states,
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(\cdot)=\sum_{i}\,(\Phi_{t}(\pi)_{i})^{-1}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|
and
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}(\cdot)=\sum_{i}\,\pi_{i}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|
irrespective of
f
and
f^{\prime}
. Then, putting this elements together one obtains Eq. (
76
). This can be interpreted as the Bayes rule by thinking of
\pi_{i}
as the probability of obtaining the microstate
i
,
(\Phi_{t}(\pi))_{j}
as the probability to be in the microstate
j
after the evolution
\Phi_{t}
and, finally,
(\Phi_{t})_{j,i}
as the conditional probability of the transition
i\rightarrow j
.

Condition
2
tells us that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
is trace preserving if
\pi
is full-rank (which we always assume to be the case in this work). This can be readily verified by applying its adjoint on the identity matrix:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi})^{\dagger}[\mathbbm{1}]=\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\,\Phi_{t}\;\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}[\mathbbm{1}]=\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(\pi)]=\mathbbm{1}\,,

(90)

where we used the fact that for every map
\Phi
the condition of being trace preserving can be written as
\Phi^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1})=\mathbbm{1}
.

Let us now pass to condition
3
. This gives a first characterisation of the spectral properties of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}
. First, it should be noticed that this map is self-adjoint with respect to
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
, as it can be readily verified:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(B)]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{t}(A)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(B)]\right]=

(91)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\Phi^{\dagger}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\right)\Phi_{t}(A)\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[B]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(A)\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[B]\right]\,,

(92)

where we repeatedly used the self-adjointness of the Fisher superoperators to move them from right to left. This shows that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}
is self-adjoint, so its spectrum is real. Moreover, it is also positive since it can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\pi}\left((\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\Phi_{t}\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\pi})^{\dagger}(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\Phi_{t}\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\pi}\right)\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big|_{\pi}\,.

(93)

This proves the claim, as similarity transformations preserve the spectrum.

It is also easy to see that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}
retrieves the prior (condition
4
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}^{\dagger}\;\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(\pi)]=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}[\Phi_{t}^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1})]=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}[\mathbbm{1}]=\pi\,.

(94)

This equation shows that the evolution of the state
\pi
can be completely undone by applying
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
. Moreover, the spectrum of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
contains
1
, and the associated eigenoperator is
\pi
.

We can now show that, with some restrictions on
f
and
f^{\prime}
, condition
5
is satisfied, namely the fact that the spectrum is actually contained in the interval
[0,1]
. Indeed, this follows from the chain of inequalities:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}(A)]\right]}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]}=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{t}(A)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}[\Phi_{t}(A)]\right]}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]}\,\leq\,\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]}\,\leq\,\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]}=1\,,

(95)

where the first inequality follows from the contractivity of the Fisher information, while the second only holds if
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\leq\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
, corresponding to the case in which
f^{\prime}(x)\leq f(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(see Eq. (
149
)).

Define now the map
P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t}):=\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}
. Condition
6
tells us that
P_{(f,f^{\prime}),\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t}))=\Phi_{t}
. This can be verified by a direct computation:

\displaystyle P_{(f,f^{\prime}),\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}(\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi})^{\dagger}\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\big|^{(f^{\prime},f)}_{\pi}\Phi_{t}(\pi)}=

(96)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}\,\Phi_{t}\;\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\right)\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}=\Phi_{t}\,.

(97)

Interestingly, this make the transformation
P_{(f,f),\pi}
involutive, since it can be reversed by just changing the base-point, i.e., through
P_{(f,f),\Phi_{t}(\pi)}
. This is one of the key properties of the classical Bayes retrieval (as it was discussed in
Surace and Scandi 2023
). Moreover, if one also requires
P_{(f,f),\pi}(\Phi_{t})
to be CP for general maps, this constrains the defining function to
f_{SQ}(x)=\sqrt{x}
, as it is the only case in which both
\mathbb{J}_{f_{SQ}}\big|_{\pi}
and
\mathbb{J}_{f_{SQ}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
are CP for any state. This gives a way to single out the usual Petz recovery map
Petz 1986a
.

Finally, condition
7
explains the relation between the generalised Petz recovery and Markovian evolutions. In particular, for divisible dynamics one has:

\displaystyle P_{(f^{\prime},f),\pi}(\Phi_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\,(\Phi_{t,s}\circ\Phi_{s})^{\dagger}\;\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t}(\pi)}=\left(\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\pi}\Phi_{s}^{\dagger}\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{s}(\pi)}\right)\left(\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime\prime}}\big|_{\Phi_{s}(\pi)}\Phi_{t,s}^{\dagger}\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi_{t,s}\Phi_{s}(\pi)}\right)=

(98)

\displaystyle=P_{(f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}),\pi}(\Phi_{s})\circ P_{(f^{\prime\prime},f),\Phi_{s}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t,s})\,,

(99)

proving the claim. It should be noticed that in the case of
f\equiv f^{\prime}
the composition becomes
P_{(f,f^{\prime\prime}),\pi}(\Phi_{s})\circ P_{(f^{\prime\prime},f),\Phi_{s}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t,s})
for any
f^{\prime\prime}
, and in particular if one choses
f^{\prime\prime}=f
it shows that
P_{(f,f),\pi}
is compatible with the structure of divisible semigroups in the sense that
P_{(f,f),\pi}(\Phi_{t})=P_{(f,f),\pi}(\Phi_{s})\circ P_{(f,f),\Phi_{s}(\pi)}(\Phi_{t,s})
.

Box 9. Generalised universal recovery maps
\leftarrow
,
\rightarrow

We discuss here a possible generalisation of the result in
Junge et al. 2018
, namely the fact that the contraction of the relative entropy (defined in Eq. (
256
)) can be bounded as:

\displaystyle S(\rho||\sigma)-S(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))\geq-\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\;\beta(t)\log F(\rho,\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}\Phi(\rho))\,,

(100)

where
\beta(t)
is a symmetric probability distribution (we refer to
Junge et al. 2018
for its particular expression),
\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}:=\mathcal{V}_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{2}-it\right)\circ\Phi^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(\sigma)}\left(it-\frac{1}{2}\right)
is called the rotated Petz recovery map, and we introduced the fidelity
F(\rho,\sigma):=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho}\,\sigma\sqrt{\rho}}\right]
. The interest in this bound is twofold: on the one hand it quantifies the minimum amount of information lost when one applies the channel
\Phi
; on the other, it implies that if
S(\rho||\sigma)=S(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
, then
\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}\Phi(\rho)=\rho
, i.e.,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}
perfectly recovers every state for which the dynamics does not decrease the relative entropy (with respect to
\sigma
). This condition is what one refers to as universality in this context. This can be proved as follows: first, it should be noticed that for any two states the fidelity
F(\rho,\sigma)
is in
[0,1]
, so its logarithm is negative definite. Hence, in this case we have the chain of inequalities:

\displaystyle 0=S(\rho||\sigma)-S(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))\geq-\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\;\beta(t)\log F(\rho,\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}\Phi(\rho))\geq 0\,,

(101)

which implies that
F(\rho,\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}\Phi(\rho))=1
for all
t
. This is only possible if
\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\sigma,t}\Phi(\rho)=\rho
, proving the claim.

One possibility of generalising Eq. (
100
) is to try to find a similar result holding for the general family of contrast functions defined in Eq. (
3
). Unfortunately, to the best of our knowledge this problem is still open. Nevertheless, if one focuses on
\chi^{2}_{f}
-divergences alone (see the definition in Eq. (
34
)), we can prove that:

Theorem 10
.

Given a CPTP map
\Phi
and any two standard monotone functions
f
and
f^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}(x)\leq f(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{f^{\prime}}(\rho||\sigma)-\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Delta\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}\left[(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)\right]\right]\geq

(102)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}_{f^{\prime},\rho}\left((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)\right)\geq\left|\left|(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)\right|\right|_{1}^{2}\,,

(103)

where we used the abbreviation
\Delta=(\rho-\sigma)
and introduced the trace norm
\left|\left|A\right|\right|_{1}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[|A|\right]
.

There are a number of remarks to be made: first, it should be noticed that the relative entropy cannot be expressed in terms of
\chi^{2}_{f}
-divergences, so the result in Thm.
10
is not a direct generalisation of Eq. (
100
). Nonetheless, it is a first step in this direction. Moreover, one should also notice the crucial role of the map
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)
: this was the key ingredient in proving Thm.
9
and it is now again at the centre of the above result. This further corroborates the interpretation of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
as a retrieval map: indeed, the quality of the retrodiction can be assessed by comparing
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi
(the map forward and backwards) with
\mathds{I}
(not doing anything). The fact that the operator
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)
naturally appears in many different constructions shows that this interpretation is well justified. Finally, we also point out that a similar result as Thm.
10
was found in
Gao et al. 2023
(Lemma 4.9 therein).

Proof.

The first equality in Eq. (
102
) directly follows from the definition of generalised Petz recovery map in Eq. (
75
):

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{f^{\prime}}(\rho||\sigma)-\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Delta\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}[\Delta]\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi(\Delta)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\rho)}[\Phi(\Delta)]\right]=

(104)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Delta\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}[\Delta]\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Delta\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}[\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi(\Delta)]\right]=

(105)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Delta\,\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}\left[(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)\right]\right]\,,

(106)

where we used the same definition of
\Delta
as in the theorem. At this point we can use the property
5
once more, which says that the spectrum of
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)
is in
[0,1]
for
f
and
f^{\prime}
as in the statement of the theorem. But from this it follows that
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)\geq(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi)^{2}
, which directly implies the first inequality in Eq. (
103
). Finally, since for any
f
it holds that
\mathcal{F}_{f,\rho}(\delta\rho)\geq\mathcal{F}_{B,\rho}(\delta\rho)\geq\left|\left|\delta\rho\right|\right|_{1}^{2}
(see Sec.
V.1
where
\mathcal{F}_{B,\rho}(\delta\rho)
is defined) the last inequality also follows.
∎

We are now ready to generalise the result in Eq. (
101
):

Corollary 10.1
.

Suppose that
\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)=\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
for some standard monotone function
f
. Then, the map
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\rho)=\rho\qquad\land\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\sigma\,.

(107)

Moreover, if
\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)=\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
for
f_{SQ}(x)=\sqrt{x}
, then the equality holds also for all other
\chi^{2}_{f}
-divergences.

Proof.

From Thm.
10
it directly follows that:

\displaystyle 0=\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)-\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))\geq\mathcal{F}_{f,\rho}\left((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)\right)\geq 0\,,

(108)

which implies
\mathcal{F}_{f,\rho}\left((\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)\right)=0
. Since the Fisher information arises from a non-degenerate scalar product, this means that
(\mathds{I}-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi)(\Delta)=0

\implies

\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\Delta)=\Delta
. It follows from property
4
that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\rho)=\rho
. Hence, the only way for
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi
to retrieve
\Delta=(\rho-\sigma)
is that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\sigma
, proving the first part of the claim.

As it was mentioned in
Box 8
, the case
f_{SQ}(x)=\sqrt{x}
is the only one in which
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f_{SQ},f_{SQ}),\rho}
is CP in general (in the following we will denote this quantity by
\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\rho}
). Then, if
\chi^{2}_{f_{SQ}}(\rho||\sigma)=\chi^{2}_{f_{SQ}}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
, this implies the existence of a channel recovering both
\rho
and
\sigma
. Then, by using the contractivity of
\chi^{2}_{f}
-divergences it follows that:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)\geq\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))\geq\chi^{2}_{f}(\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\rho}\Phi(\rho)||\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\rho}\Phi(\sigma))=\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)\,.

(109)

This directly implies that
\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)=\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
.
∎

The proof above shows that the exceptionality of the square root divergence derives from the fact that this is the only case in which
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}
is a channel. Moreover, allowing the two defining functions
f
and
f^{\prime}
to be different does not help in general, as the following corollary shows:

Corollary 10.2
.

Suppose that
\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)=\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
for some standard monotone function
f
. Then, choosing
f^{\prime}\neq f
, the map
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi(\rho)=\rho\qquad\land\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\sigma\,.

(110)

if and only if
[\rho,\sigma]=0
. In this case, the equality for any
\chi^{2}_{f}
-divergence such that
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}
is CP implies the equality for all other
\chi^{2}_{f^{\prime}}
-divergences.

Proof.

It should be noticed that the requirement
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi(\rho)=\rho
directly follows from the property
4
, so it can be imposed without problems. Let us now focus on the second part of Eq. (
110
), namely:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\rho}\,\Phi^{\dagger}\;\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\rho)}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\left(\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\rho}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}\right)\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\rho}\,\Phi^{\dagger}\;\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\rho)}\right)\,\Phi(\sigma)=

(111)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\rho}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}\right)\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\rho}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}[\sigma]\,,

(112)

where in the second line we used Corollary
10.1
to substitute
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f,f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\sigma
. Now, if
f\neq f^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\rho}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}[\sigma]=\sigma
if and only if
[\rho,\sigma]=0
, as it can be verified in coordinates (see Sec.
IV
).

Regarding the second part of the Corollary suppose that there exists a standard monotone
f
such that
\chi^{2}_{f}(\rho||\sigma)=\chi^{2}_{f}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))
and
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}
is CP. Then, by choosing any
f^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}
is CP,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
is a quantum channel. Moreover, if
[\rho,\sigma]=0
, it follows from Eq. (
112
) that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\,\Phi(\sigma)=\sigma
. Thus, for any
\chi^{2}_{\tilde{f}}
-divergence it holds that:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{\tilde{f}}(\rho||\sigma)\geq\chi^{2}_{\tilde{f}}(\Phi(\rho)||\Phi(\sigma))\geq\chi^{2}_{\tilde{f}}(\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi(\rho)||\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi(\sigma))=\chi^{2}_{\tilde{f}}(\rho||\sigma)\,,

(113)

proving the claim.
∎

In the result above it should be noticed that even if one needs the commutativity of
\rho
and
\sigma
, their evolved versions
\Phi(\rho)
and
\Phi(\sigma)
can be general. Still, it should be noticed that if one requires
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
to be a channel, the only unconstrained recovery result of the form in Eq. (
110
) is given by the Petz recovery map, i.e., for
f(x)=f^{\prime}(x)=\sqrt{x}
.

Finally, we provide yet another way of singling out the Petz recovery map from the generalised family
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
. Indeed, this one corresponds to the best recovery in a specific sense:

Theorem 11
.

Consider the family of positive superoperators
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi
such that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
is CP. One can introduce the partial order
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi\geq\widetilde{\Phi}_{(\tilde{f}^{\prime},\tilde{f}),\rho}\Phi\iff(\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi-\widetilde{\Phi}_{(\tilde{f}^{\prime},\tilde{f}),\rho}\Phi)\geq 0
. Then, there is a unique supremum given by:

\displaystyle\sup_{f,f^{\prime}}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi=\widetilde{\Phi}_{P,\rho}\,\Phi\,.

(114)

This results means that among all possible recovery channels
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
, the Petz is the one that maximises the spectrum of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi
.

Proof.

As it was mentioned in
Box 8
, if
f(x)\leq f^{\prime}(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, this implies that
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\rho}\leq\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}\big|_{\rho}
(see Eq. (
149
)). Moreover, a necessary condition for
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\rho}
to be CP is that
f(x)\leq\sqrt{x}
, so one can maximise
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
by choosing the maximum
f^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{f^{\prime}}\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}\Phi=\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f_{SQ},f),\rho}\Phi\,,

(115)

where we denote by
f_{SQ}(x)=\sqrt{x}
. On the other hand, for
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}\geq\mathbb{J}_{f^{\prime}}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}
if and only if
f(x)\leq f^{\prime}(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Moreover, a necessary condition for
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}
to be CP is that
f(x)\geq\sqrt{x}
. Hence, the maximum
\widetilde{\Phi}_{(f^{\prime},f),\rho}
in this case is reached for the minimum function
f
, that is once again for
f_{SQ}
. Finally, the fact that the two supremum are independent of each other proves the claim.
∎

III.3
Fisher information and detailed balance

In this section we explain how the notion of detailed balanced evolutions can be given in terms of self-adjointness with respect to the Fisher information scalar product. To this end, it is useful to start with the study of the classical case to avoid the complications arising from the variety of different quantum Fisher information.

Classical case.

As discussed in
Box 6
, the dynamics of a classical divisible evolution
\Phi_{t}
is described by a stochastic matrix, which can be equivalently characterised in terms of its rate matrices
R_{s}
(
\forall s\leq t
, see Eq. (
65
)), which induce the dynamics:

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\Phi_{t}(\rho)=R_{t}(\rho)\,,

(116)

where
\rho
is in this case a diagonal matrix.
As it was mentioned in
Box 6
, rate matrices can be generically decomposed as:

\displaystyle R_{t}=\sum_{i\neq j}\;a^{(t)}_{i\leftarrow j}\left(\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|-\left.\left|{j}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\right)\,,

(117)

where
a^{(t)}_{i\leftarrow j}
are real coefficients that are non-negative for Markovian evolutions. In this context, one can formulate the condition of detailed balanced evolution (with respect to some state
\pi
) in terms of the rates alone:

\displaystyle a^{(t)}_{i\leftarrow j}\,\pi_{j}=a^{(t)}_{j\leftarrow i}\,\pi_{i}\,,

(118)

where the equation above holds for every
i
and
j
. This condition directly implies that
\pi
is a steady state, as it can be readily verified by a straightforward computation.
Indeed, detailed balance corresponds to a stronger notion of equilibration: not only the dynamics has
\pi
as a fixed points, but it is also time symmetric at equilibrium. In fact, if one interprets the rates
a^{(t)}_{j\leftarrow i}
as the probability per unit of time of the transition
j\leftarrow i
, Eq. (
118
) can be read as the condition that the probability of observing the transition
j\leftarrow i
is equal to the one for the reverse transition
j\rightarrow i
, when the system is at equilibrium. For this reason, detailed balance encodes the request of microscopic reversibility of the general dynamics, i.e., the fact that at a molecular level the equations of motion are time symmetric.

We show now that Eq. (
118
) can be naturally formulated in terms of the Fisher information scalar product. In analogy with the quantum case (Eq. (
21
)), we use the notation for the scalar product:

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(\delta\rho,\delta\sigma):=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}[\delta\sigma]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho\,\delta\sigma\,\pi^{-1}\right]\,,

(119)

where we implicitly defined
\mathcal{J}_{\pi}
to be the component-wise multiplication by
\pi
, and all the operators involved commute. This scalar product naturally emerges when one is considering variations of states (as proved in Thm.
1
), thus from a differential geometric point of view, the two vectors
\delta\rho
and
\delta\sigma
should be elements of the tangent space of the state space (i.e., Hermitian, traceless operators and, in this case, diagonal).

Moreover, one can also interpret
\mathcal{J}_{\pi}
as the scalar product on the cotangent space, i.e., the space of observables: indeed, a metric on the tangent space naturally induces one on its dual by taking the pointwise matrix inverse
O’Neill 1983
. Hence, one can also define the Fisher scalar product on the space of observables as:

\displaystyle{K}^{o}_{\pi}(A,B):=\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}[B]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,B\,\pi\right]\,,

(120)

where in this case
A
and
B
are not required to be traceless. It should be noticed that quantities analogous to
{K}^{o}_{\pi}(A,B)
naturally emerge in statistical mechanics and in linear response theory when studying two-point correlation functions.

Since the definition of the adjoint of a superoperator
\Phi
depends on the underlying scalar product, it is useful to present its expression when considering
K_{\pi}
. Then, in this context it satisfies the property:

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(\delta\rho,\Phi(\delta\sigma))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}[\Phi(\delta\sigma)]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}[\delta\rho]\Phi(\delta\sigma)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}[\delta\rho]\,\delta\sigma\right]=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathcal{J}_{\pi}\,\Phi^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}\right)(\delta\rho)\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}[\delta\sigma]\right]=K_{\pi}(\widetilde{\Phi}(\delta\rho),\delta\sigma)\,,

(121)

where we implicitly defined
\widetilde{\Phi}:=\mathcal{J}_{\pi}\,\Phi^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}
. Since the equation above holds for any
\delta\rho
and
\delta\sigma
in the tangent space, we can identify
\widetilde{\Phi}
with the adjoint of
\Phi
with respect to the Fisher information metric. Then, self-adjointness in this context takes the form
\widetilde{\Phi}=\Phi
, which can be rewritten as
\Phi\circ\mathcal{J}_{\pi}=\mathcal{J}_{\pi}\circ\Phi^{\dagger}
. In coordinates this condition reads:

\displaystyle\Phi_{i,j}\,\pi_{j}={\Phi}_{j,i}\,\pi_{i}\,,

(122)

which has a striking similarity with Eq. (
118
). Indeed, it follows from Eq. (
122
) that the requirement that
R_{t}
is detailed balanced exactly means that
\widetilde{R}_{t}=R_{t}
, i.e., the generator of the dynamics is self-adjoint with respect to the Fisher information scalar product.

This result was derived in the Schrödinger picture, meaning that the states are the only evolving objects, while observables are static quantities. The dual situation, dubbed Heisenberg picture, is the one in which states are fixed in time, while the whole dynamics is relegated to observables. In this case it is well known that the generator of the dynamics is given by
R_{t}^{\dagger}
. Moreover, as it was argued above, the natural Fisher scalar product is the one given by
{K}^{o}_{\pi}
. We denote the adjoint with respect to this scalar product by
\widetilde{\Phi}^{o}:=\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}\,\Phi^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}
, where this condition can be verified carrying out calculations completely analogous to the one that led to Eq. (
121
). It is easy to verify that:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}=\Phi\qquad\iff\qquad(\widetilde{\Phi^{\dagger}})^{o}=\Phi^{\dagger}\,,

(123)

which implies that
R_{t}
is
K_{\pi}
-self-adjoint if and only if
R_{t}^{\dagger}
is
K^{o}_{\pi}
-self-adjoint. This shows that one can formulate the condition of being detailed balanced both in the Schrödinger and in the Heisenberg picture, resorting only to the use of Fisher scalar products.

Putting these results together we obtain the theorem:

Theorem 12
.

The following conditions are equivalent in the classical case:

1.

the dynamics is detailed balanced, i.e., the rate matrix is characterised by coefficients satisfying

\displaystyle a^{(t)}_{i\leftarrow j}\,\pi_{j}=a^{(t)}_{j\leftarrow i}\,\pi_{i}\,;

(124)

2.

the rate matrix
R_{t}
is self-adjoint with respect to the Fisher scalar product:

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}_{t}=R_{t}\,;

(125)

3.

the rate matrix in the Heisenberg picture (i.e.,
R_{t}^{\dagger}
) is self-adjoint with respect to the dual Fisher metric:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{R_{t}^{\dagger}})^{o}=R_{t}^{\dagger}\,.

(126)

This characterisation shows how the condition of being detailed balanced directly corresponds to the self-adjointness of the generator of the dynamics with respect to a properly defined scalar products. With this hindsight in mind, we can now pass to the quantum regime.

Quantum case.

Consider now the dynamics induced by the Lindbladian operator in the form in Eq. (
49
), i.e.:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\rho]=-i[H,\rho]+\sum_{\alpha}^{d^{2}}\;\lambda_{\alpha}\,\left(A_{\alpha}\,\rho\,A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{A_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\,A_{\alpha},\rho\}\right)\,,

(127)

where we dropped the time dependence to simplify the notation. Since the Fisher information is invariant under the action of a purely Hamiltonian dynamics, whereas it is contracting otherwise, we split the two terms in the Lindbladian by introducing the notation
\mathcal{U}(\rho):=-i[H,\rho]
, and we call
dissipator
the difference
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}:=\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{U}
. It should be noticed that
\mathcal{U}
is skew-Hermitian with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt scalar product, meaning that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathcal{U}(B)\right]=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathcal{U}(A)\,B\right]\,.

(128)

Given the structure of the Lindbladian above, we can introduce the notion of quantum detailed balance. Historically, one of the first formalisations of this notion was provided by Alicki in
Alicki 1976
, and it is based on the following scalar product on the space of observables:

\displaystyle{K}^{o}_{\pi}(A,B):=\mathrm{Tr}\left[AB\,\pi\right]\,,

(129)

where in this case
A
,
B
and
\pi
are not required to commute in general (unlike in Eq. (
120
)). Similarly to the case of classical systems, this scalar product is quite natural as it is related to two-points correlation functions, but it should also be kept in mind that it is not part of the Fisher family. Using the same notation as in the classical case, we denote by
\widetilde{\Phi}^{o}
the adjoint of the map
\Phi
with respect to the scalar product in Eq. (
129
). Then, the definition proposed by Alicki reads:

Definition 1

(Heisenberg picture
Alicki 1976
)
.

The dynamics generated in the Heisenberg picture by the operator
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
is detailed balanced if the three conditions are satisfied:

1.

\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
is normal with respect to the scalar product
{K}^{o}_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}^{\dagger},(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}})^{o}]=0\,;

(130)

2.

the commutator
\mathcal{U}
is skew-Hermitian with respect to
{K}^{o}_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{o}=-\mathcal{U}\,;

(131)

3.

the dissipator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}
is self-adjoint with respect to
{K}^{o}_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}})^{o}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}\,.

(132)

Interestingly, from this definition one can deduce a structural characterisation of detailed balanced Lindbladians (see
Alicki 1976
), which explicitly reads:

Definition 2

(Structural definition)
.

The dynamics generated by the Lindbladian operator
\mathcal{L}
satisfies detailed balance if its diagonal form can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\rho)=-i[H,\rho]+\sum_{\omega,i}\;\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\left(A_{i}^{\omega}\,\rho\,(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}A_{i}^{\omega},\rho\}\right)\,,

(133)

and the following conditions hold:

1.

[H,\pi]=0
;

2.

(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}=A_{i}^{-\omega}
;

3.

\pi\,A_{i}^{\omega}\,\pi^{-1}=e^{\omega}\,A_{i}^{\omega}
;

4.

\lambda_{i}^{\omega}=e^{\omega}\,\lambda_{i}^{-\omega}
.

In the literature one usually finds Def.
2
as the usual definition of detailed balance, as it mirrors the same structural properties we saw for classical systems
Breuer et al. 2002
. Indeed, from the condition
3
we know that
A_{i}^{\omega}
are eigenoperators of the modular operator
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, with eigenvalue
e^{\omega}
. On the other hand, all the eigenvalues of
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
are of the form
\pi_{i}/\pi_{j}
, so the only values of
\omega
that are allowed are the ones satisfying the constraint
e^{\omega}=\pi_{i}/\pi_{j}
for some
i
and
j
. Then, by substituting this expression into condition
4
, one obtains the analogous characterisation at the level of the rates we found for classical detailed balanced dynamics (see Eq. (
124
)).

Despite this positive result, it should be noticed that the choice of the scalar product in Eq. (
129
) is somehow arbitrary, as in the passage from commuting observables to the non-commuting case there are many different possible orderings that can be used to extend the multiplication operator
\mathcal{J}_{\pi}
. Moreover, it should be noticed that the scalar product
{K}^{o}_{\pi}
in Eq. (
129
) is not monotone under CP-maps, a property that its classical counterpart had. For these reasons, in the following we show how one can define detailed balance through the help of quantum Fisher information metrics.

A first possible definition is given by imposing
\widetilde{\Phi}_{f}=\Phi
for some Fisher scalar product
K_{f,\pi}
(see Eq. (
21
)), where we simplified the notation from the one used in the generalised Petz map
4
4

4

The reason why we do not consider the more general definition
\widetilde{\Phi}^{(f^{\prime},f)}
is that the adjoint operation should be involutive in finite dimensions, that is the adjoint of the adjoint should give back the original map. As explained in
Box 8
this is only true when
f^{\prime}\equiv f
.
defined in Eq. (
75
), i.e.,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{f}:=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\,\Phi_{t}^{\dagger}\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
. As it could be expected, the structure induced is much richer in this case. In fact, one can show that different
K_{f,\pi}
induce inequivalent notions of detailed balance, by constructing dynamics that are detailed balanced with respect to one
f
but not with respect to others
Temme et al. 2010
. Moreover, these conditions are all weaker than the one provided by Def.
1
. This means that dynamics that are detailed balanced with respect to
K_{\pi}^{o}
are also detailed balanced with respect with
K_{f,\pi}
, but not the other way round.
Since in principle there is no preferred definition of quantum Fisher information, we choose to impose the condition of detailed balance for all the
K_{f,\pi}
at the same time:

Definition 3

(Schroedinger picture)
.

The dynamics generated by the Lindbladian operator
\mathcal{L}
is detailed balance if for every standard monotone function
f
the following holds:

1.

\mathcal{L}
is normal with respect to all the scalar products
K_{f,\pi}
:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{f}]=0\,;

(134)

2.

the commutator
\mathcal{U}
is skew-Hermitian with respect to
K_{f,\pi}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{f}=-\mathcal{U}\,;

(135)

3.

the dissipator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
is self-adjoint with respect to
K_{f,\pi}
:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}})_{f}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\,.

(136)

By this point we have three different definitions of what it means for a Markovian dynamics to be detailed balance. Then, we can wrap everything together, providing a characterisation of their interdependency:

Theorem 13
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

the generator of the dynamics in the Heisenberg picture
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
satisfies the adjointness relations in Def.
1
;

2.

the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the structural characterisation in Def.
2
.

These conditions imply the condition:

3.

the generator of the dynamics in the Schroedinger picture
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the adjointness relations in Def.
3
.

Moreover, if the Hamiltonian
H
is non-degenerate the three conditions are equivalent.

The proof of this result is provided in App.
C
. This shows that the definition based on Fisher scalar products is weaker (i.e., it includes a larger set of dynamics) even when taking into consideration all the possible scalar products at the same time. This should be contrasted with the definition by Alicki, in which a single scalar product is used. The difference between the two arises in the way in which coherences in the eigenbasis of
\pi
are handled. Still, it should be noticed that in both cases the evolution induced by the unitary part decouples from the dissipative dynamics. In fact, thanks to the normality of the generator, one has:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}},-\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}]=0\qquad\implies\qquad[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}]=0\,.

(137)

This generic property can be used to further constrain
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
in the case in which
H
is non-degenerate, allowing for the identification of Def.
1
and Def.
3
in this case.

If
H
is degenerate, on the other hand, we can provide the following structural characterisation of dissipators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
that are detailed balanced according to Def.
3
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}[\rho]=\sum_{\omega,i}\;\left(\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\left(A_{i}^{\omega}\,\rho\,(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}A_{i}^{\omega},\rho\}\right)+\mu^{\omega}_{i}\,B_{i}^{\omega}\,\rho^{T}(B_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}\right)\,,

(138)

where the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}=A_{i}^{-\omega}
and
(B_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}=B_{i}^{-\omega}
;

2.

\pi\,A_{i}^{\omega}\,\pi^{-1}=e^{\omega}\,A_{i}^{\omega}
and
\pi\,B_{i}^{\omega}\,\pi^{-1}=e^{\omega}\,B_{i}^{\omega}
;

3.

\lambda_{i}^{\omega}=e^{\omega}\,\lambda_{i}^{-\omega}
and
\mu_{i}^{\omega}=e^{\omega}\,\mu_{i}^{-\omega}
;

4.

\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}^{\omega}=0

\forall\omega
.

We defer the proof to App.
C.3
. It should be noticed that the characterisation in Eq. (
138
) clearly generalises Def.
2
, as the first can be considered a restriction of the latter to the case in which
\mu_{i}^{\omega}=0
for all
i
and
\omega
. Moreover, it should also be pointed out that, thanks to the second part of condition
4
, the extra term only acts on off-diagonal elements, i.e, it only affects the dynamics of the coherences.

IV
Mathematical properties of quantum Fisher information

The aim of this section is to explore the main properties of the superoperator
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
, whose definition we repeat here for convenience:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}:=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}\,f\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}\,.

(139)

Before doing so, however, it is useful to study the properties of the set of standard monotone functions, which we will denote by
\mathcal{M}
.

IV.1
The set of standard monotone functions

As it was discussed in Sec.
II
, every
f\in\mathcal{M}
satisfies the following three properties:

1.

is matrix monotone;

2.

is Fisher adjusted, meaning that
f(1)=1
;

3.

satisfies the symmetry
{f(x)=xf(x^{-1})}
.

In
Box 2
it was shown that one can pointwise bound all the standard monotones as:

\displaystyle\frac{2x}{x+1}\,\leq f(x)\leq\,\frac{x+1}{2}\,,

(140)

which automatically proves that all
f\in\mathcal{M}
are bounded for
x\in[0,1]
, and diverge as
x\rightarrow\infty
at most in a linear manner. We denote by
f_{H}(x)
and
f_{B}(x)
the smallest and the largest standard monotone functions. Thanks to the pointwise characterisation of Eq. (
140
), it is then natural to introduce the partial order on
\mathcal{M}
defined by
f_{1}\leq f_{2}
if and only if
f_{1}(x)\leq f_{2}(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Then, every
f\in\mathcal{M}
satisfies
f_{H}\leq\,f\,\leq f_{B}
(simply rewriting Eq. (
140
)), but it should be noticed that this is not an if and only if condition: there are functions for which Eq. (
140
) holds but that are not standard monotones
5
5

5

One can easily construct examples of this: consider for instance the function
f(x):=\cos(x+x^{-1})^{2}\,f_{B}(x)+\sin(x+x^{-1})^{2}f_{H}(x)
. By construction it is Fisher adjusted, satisfies the symmetry condition
f(x)=xf(x^{-1})
, and satisfies
f_{H}\leq\,f\,\leq f_{B}
. Still, it is clearly not monotone, as it oscillates infinitely many times between
f_{H}
and
f_{B}
.
.

There is a fundamental symmetry of
\mathcal{M}
that was exploited to prove Eq. (
140
), given by the transformation
f(x)\rightarrow[Tf](x):=x/f(x)
. Thanks to condition
2
in Lemma
1
, this transformation maps standard monotones to standard monotones (i.e,
T:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
). Moreover,
T
is also order-reversing (
f\leq h\iff Th\leq Tf
), from which it directly follows that
Tf_{B}=f_{H}
: indeed, since for every
f
in
\mathcal{M}
one has
f\,\leq f_{B}
, then it also holds that
\forall f\in\mathcal{M}
,
Tf_{B}\,\leq Tf
, which is the defining property of
f_{H}
(this is exactly part of the derivation used in
Box 2
to prove Eq. (
140
)). Moreover, thanks to the symmetry
f(x)=xf(x^{-1})
we can rewrite the action of
T
as:

\displaystyle[Tf](x)=\frac{x}{f(x)}=\frac{1}{f(x^{-1})}\,.

(141)

From this expression one can directly verify that
T
is involutive, i.e.,
TTf=f
. One first consequence is that
Tf_{H}=TTf_{B}=f_{B}
. Moreover, this also means that we can partition
\mathcal{M}
into two sets
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}=T\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Moreover, we can choose the intersection between this two sets to be only one element, i.e., the only function for which
f=Tf
, namely
f_{SQ}(x)=\sqrt{x}
. Then, by defining
\mathcal{M}_{00}:=\mathcal{M}_{0}\setminus f_{SQ}
, we can express the set of standard monotones as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\left(\bigsqcup_{f\in\mathcal{M}_{00}}\{f,Tf\}\right)\bigsqcup\,f_{SQ}\,,

(142)

that is, as the disjoint union of sets of only two elements and, additionally, the square root function.

Thanks to the possibility of rewriting
T
as in Eq. (
141
), this transformation is also particularly useful because it allows to lift some properties of
f\in\mathcal{M}
to their multiplicative inverse, and vice versa. For example, it was proved in
Hansen 2008
that the inverse of a standard monotone can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{f(x)}=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{\lambda+1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{x+\lambda}+\frac{1}{1+\lambda\,x}\right)\,,

(143)

where
{\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)
is a probability measure on
[0,1]
. Moreover, by plugging into the integral above an arbitrary probability measure, the resulting function is always the inverse of a standard monotone. It then follows from Eq. (
141
) that we can express standard monotone functions as:

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{x}{Tf(x)}=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{Tf}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left(\frac{x}{x+\lambda}+\frac{x}{1+\lambda\,x}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{Tf}(\lambda)\;f_{\lambda}(x)\,.

(144)

This shows that
\mathcal{M}
is a convex set, which coincides with the convex hull of the extreme points denoted by
f_{\lambda}(x)
(in particular since the set of
f_{\lambda}(x)
is closed, this space has the structure of a Bauer simplex
Hiai et al. 2013
).

IV.2
Properties of quantum Fisher operators

Now that the main properties of the set
\mathcal{M}
have been discussed, we can start studying how these reflect on the operators
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
and
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
. First, let us explore the main implications of the three defining properties:

1.

the monotonicity of
f
implies that for every CPTP map
\Phi
the two inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\right)\Phi\;

\displaystyle\leq\;{\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}}\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad\Phi\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\right)\Phi^{\dagger}\;\leq\;{\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}}\,;

(145)

2.

the property of
f
to be Fisher adjusted implies that
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[A]=A\pi
and
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]=A\pi^{-1}
for every
A
such that
[A,\pi]=0
;

3.

the symmetry
{f(x)=xf(x^{-1})}
implies that
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
is adjoint-preserving, i.e., it maps self-adjoint operators into self-adjoint operators.

The proofs of these facts, and the other manipulations of the section are deferred to App.
D
.

In order to make the discussion more concrete, it is useful to give the coordinate expression of
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
in the eigenbasis of
\pi
. To this end, we express the state in coordinates as:

\displaystyle\pi=\sum_{i}\pi_{i}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{i}\right.\right|\,.

(146)

It should be noticed that the modular operator is diagonal in the basis given by
\{\left.\left|{\pi_{i}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\pi_{j}}\right.\right|\}
, as

\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}[\left.\left|{\pi_{i}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\pi_{j}}\right.\right|]=\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{j}}\left.\left|{\pi_{i}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\pi_{j}}\right.\right|
. Thanks to this, it is straightforward to compute the action of
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
on
\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|
by directly using the definition in Eq. (
139
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\left[\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\right]=f\left(\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{j}}\right)\pi_{j}\;\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\,;\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\left[\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\right]=\left(f\left(\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{j}}\right)\pi_{j}\right)^{-1}\;\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\,.

(147)

Consider now an operator
A
which can be written in coordinates as
A:=\sum A_{i,j}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|
. Then,
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
acts as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[A]=\sum_{i,j}f\left(\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{j}}\right)\pi_{j}\;A_{i,j}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|=\sum({J}_{f,\pi}\circ A)_{i,j}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|\,,

(148)

where we introduced the matrix
{J}_{f,\pi}:=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|]
, and we use the circle to denote the Hadamard product, that is a product acting as
A\circ B=\sum_{i,j}A_{i,j}B_{i,j}\left.\left|{i}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{j}\right.\right|
. Analogously, the same computation can be carried out for
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
.

Since all the
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
are diagonal in the same basis, we can lift the partial order from
\mathcal{M}
to the operators. Indeed, as it can be verified in coordinates, one has that:

\displaystyle f_{1}\leq f_{2}\quad\implies\quad\mathbb{J}_{f_{1}}\big|_{\pi}\leq\,\mathbb{J}_{f_{2}}\big|_{\pi}\,.

(149)

Hence, it also follows that for every
f\in\mathcal{M}
the corresponding operator satisfies
\mathbb{J}_{f_{H}}\big|_{\pi}\leq\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\leq\mathbb{J}_{f_{B}}\big|_{\pi}
. This allows to prove a key property of the Fisher operators: if one restricts the spectrum of
\pi
to be bounded away from zero, then
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
is a bounded operator (as a function of
\pi
). Since for every
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
it holds that
\mathbb{J}_{f_{B}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\leq\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\leq\mathbb{J}_{f_{H}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
(as the inversion reverts the inequalities), we can verify this fact just by studying the behaviour of
\mathbb{J}_{f_{B}}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
. Then, by explicitly computing the spectrum using Eq. (
147
) for
f_{B}
, which takes the form
\left\{\frac{2}{\pi_{i}+\pi_{j}}\right\}_{i,j}
, we see that this is a bounded set whenever
\pi
is bounded away from zero. This property was the key ingredient in the proof of Thm.
3
.

As it was mentioned in the previous section, the transformation
T
lifts properties of
f
to its inverse
1/f
. Indeed, the same also holds when considering Fisher information operators, thanks to the following chain of equalities:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{Tf}\big|_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}\,Tf(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}\frac{1}{f}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}\mathbb{R}_{\pi})=\mathbb{R}_{\pi^{-1}}^{-1}\frac{1}{f}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi^{-1}}\mathbb{R}_{\pi^{-1}}^{-1})=\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{f}\big|_{\pi^{-1}}\,.

(150)

This means that the properties of
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
are in one to one correspondence with the properties of
\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{Tf}\big|_{\pi^{-1}}
, fact that will be particularly relevant in the next section.

Finally, it should be noticed that the convex structure of
\mathcal{M}
also reflects on the operators
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
. For this reason, it is interesting to study the operators associated to
f_{\lambda}
, namely:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f_{\lambda}}\big|_{\pi}[A]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi})^{-1}\right)[\pi A\pi]\,,

(151)

Then, thanks to Eq. (
144
), we have that any arbitrary quantum Fisher operator
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[A]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{Tf}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi})^{-1}\right)[\pi A\pi]\,.

(152)

Moreover, using the transformation in Eq. (
150
), we also have the expression for generic
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
, namely:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{Tf}\big|_{\pi^{-1}}[A]=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi})^{-1}\right)[A]\,.

(153)

The two formulas above are derived in App.
D
. It should be noticed that the expression for
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
could also be directly inferred from the integral expansion of
1/f
in Eq. (
143
).

Box 10. Some additional properties of contrast functions
\rightarrow

Now that the quantum Fisher operators have been more thoroughly characterised, we can discuss some other properties of the contrast functions. In particular, by using their integral expression in the symmetrised case (and taking the adjoint of the second term in Eq. (
9
)) we have up to corrections of order
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)
that:

\displaystyle H^{\rm symm}_{g}(\pi+\varepsilon A||\pi+\varepsilon B)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[(A-B)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+s\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+s\mathbb{L}_{\pi})^{-1}\right)[(A-B)]\right]=

(154)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[(A-B)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[(A-B)]\right]\,,

(155)

where in the second line we simply used Thm.
1
. The expression in Eq. (
154
) should be compared with the one obtained in Eq. (
153
), which shows that the two defining measures are connected by the relation:

\displaystyle{\rm d}N_{g}(\lambda):={\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\,.

(156)

Since
{\rm d}\mu_{f}
is a probability distribution, this also means that
{\rm d}N_{g}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\frac{2}{1+s}=1\,.

(157)

Indeed, this normalisation condition could have even been verified directly from the requirement that
H^{\rm symm}_{g}(\pi+\varepsilon A||\pi+\varepsilon B)
needs to reduce to the classical Fisher information for commuting variables. Indeed, for
[A,\pi]=0
one has
(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+s\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}[A]=\frac{A\pi^{-1}}{(1+s)}
. Moreover, it also directly follows by comparing Eq. (
17
) with Eq. (
143
).

Let us define the set of standard convex functions to be the set of functions
g\in\mathcal{G}
satisfying the following three properties:

1.

g
is matrix convex;

2.

g
is Fisher adjusted, that is
g^{\prime\prime}(1)=1
;

3.

g
satisfies the symmetry
g(x)=xg(x^{-1})
.

As it was mentioned in condition
3
(Sec.
II
), the normalisation in the second requirement is chosen so that the corresponding contrast function will correctly reduce to a quantum Fisher information. Finally, since
H_{g}(\rho||\sigma)
coincides with
H_{\tilde{g}}(\sigma||\rho)
, with
\tilde{g}(x)=xg(x^{-1})
(as discussed in Eq. (
8
)), the last requirement restricts our attention to symmetric contrast functions. Then, there exists a bijective map between the set of standard convex functions and the set of standard monotones
L:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
which, thanks to Eq. (
13
), can be explicitly expressed as
Lg(x)=\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{2g(x)}
. Moreover, it is easy to verify that this transformation is involutive, meaning
L^{2}g=g
. Thanks to Eq. (
6
), together with the discussion at the beginning of the section, we can also see that
\mathcal{G}
has the structure of a Bauer simplex, with extreme points given by:

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}(x)=\left(\frac{\lambda+1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+\lambda}+\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{1+\lambda\,x}\right)\,.

(158)

It should be noticed that
L
reverses the partial order
\leq
defined on
\mathcal{M}
, meaning that
f_{1}\leq f_{2}\iff Lf_{2}\leq Lf_{1}
. This implies that there is a larger and a smaller elements in
\mathcal{G}
, respectively corresponding to
Lf_{H}
and
Lf_{B}
. In formulae, this reads:

\displaystyle\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+1}\,\leq\,g(x)\,\leq\,\frac{(x+1)(x-1)^{2}}{4x}\,.

(159)

Interestingly, the same ordering is also present for symmetrised contrast functions. Indeed, we have that for every
g\in\mathcal{G}
it holds that (see App.
E
):

\displaystyle H^{\rm symm}_{Lf_{B}}(\rho||\sigma)\leq H_{g}^{\rm symm}(\rho||\sigma)\leq H_{Lf_{H}}^{\rm symm}(\rho||\sigma)\,,

(160)

where we highlighted the fact that the contrast functions considered here are symmetric.

Finally, it should be noticed that we can lift the transformation
T:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
to
\tilde{T}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
by requiring the following diagram to commute:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{T}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mathcal{M}\;.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}

Thanks to the involutivity of
L
, this is given by
\tilde{T}:=LTL
, which explicitly reads:

\displaystyle[\tilde{T}g](x):=\frac{(x-1)^{4}}{4\,x\,g(x)}\,.

(161)

The procedure just presented exemplifies a general method to lift transformations from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{G}
and vice-versa.

IV.3
Complete positivity of the Fisher information functionals

A linear map is physically realisable only if it is completely positive. For this reason, it is particularly relevant to give a characte
risation of the cases in which
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
or its inverse are CP. To this end, we introduce the two sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{+}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{M}\,|\,\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\,\text{is CP }\forall\,\pi\right\}\,;\qquad\qquad\mathcal{M}^{-}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{M}\,|\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\,\text{is CP }\forall\,\pi\right\}\,.

(162)

Before starting with the exploration of this two sets, it should be noticed that in the context of Fisher operators positivity implies complete positivity. In fact,
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
is CP means that
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\otimes\mathds{I}_{n}
is P for any
n
. Then, thanks to the identity
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\otimes\mathds{I}_{n}=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{n}}
, if
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
is P for all states (and all dimensions), it automatically follows that it will also be CP, proving the claim.

The expression in Eq. (
148
) is particularly useful in this context because it says that one can interpret the application of
\mathbb{J}_{f}|_{\pi}
to a state
\rho
as the Hadamard product of
J_{f,\pi}
with
\rho
. Then, thanks to Schur product theorem
Hiai and Petz 2014
, the resulting matrix is positive if both
J_{f,\pi}
and
\rho
are positive (the latter is true as
\rho
is a state). Since this should hold for any
\rho
, it directly follows that
\mathbb{J}_{f}|_{\pi}
is CP if and only if
J_{f,\pi}\geq 0
.

Following the discussion above, it is easy to find that a necessary condition for
f\in\mathcal{M^{+}}
is that
f\leq f_{SQ}
. Indeed, since every principal sub-matrix of a positive matrix is also positive semidefinite, imposing the positivity of the determinant for the
2\times 2
matrix containing only the
(i,j)
-components of
J_{f,\pi}
, namely
\pi_{i}\pi_{j}-f(\pi_{i}/\pi_{j})^{2}\pi_{j}^{2}\geq 0
, we obtain the condition
f(\pi_{i}/\pi_{j})\leq\sqrt{\pi_{i}/\pi_{j}}
. Moreover, from the fact that this should hold for any non-zero probability vector
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, one can deduce that a necessary condition for
\mathbb{J}_{f}|_{\pi}
to be positive preserving is that
f(x)\leq\sqrt{x}
for every
x
. A similar argument can also be given for
\mathcal{M}^{-}
, showing that if
f\in\mathcal{M}^{-}
then
f_{SQ}\leq f
.

It should be noticed though that the one just presented is not a sufficient condition. Indeed, if one considers the family of extremal functions
f_{\lambda}
defined in Eq. (
144
), it holds that
f_{\lambda}\leq f_{SQ}
for all
\lambda\in[3-2\sqrt{2},1]
. Still, it has been proved in
Hiai et al. 2013
that for all
\lambda\neq 1

f_{\lambda}\notin\mathcal{M}^{+}
. Moreover, we can deduce another interesting property of the set of monotones from the inspection of its extreme points: for any
\lambda\in(0,3-2\sqrt{2})
the corresponding function has two additional crossing with the graph of the square root (other than at
x=0
and
x=1
) implying that
f_{\lambda}
neither lays all beneath nor all above
f_{SQ}
. Since these are necessary condition for
f_{\lambda}
to be in
\mathcal{M}^{+}
or
\mathcal{M}^{-}
, this remark implies that
\mathcal{M}\neq\mathcal{M}^{+}\cup\mathcal{M}^{-}
, that is, there are standard monotone functions for which neither
\mathbb{J}_{f}|_{\pi}
or
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}|_{\pi}
are CP.

It was shown in Eq. (
142
) that we could partition
\mathcal{M}
using the transformation
T
, and that this could be used to express
\mathbb{J}_{f}|_{\pi}
in terms of
\mathbb{J}_{Tf}^{-1}|_{\pi^{-1}}
(see Eq. (
150
)). Thanks to this last property it follows that
T\mathcal{M}^{+}=\mathcal{M}^{-}
. Indeed, suppose
f\in\mathcal{M}^{+}
. Then,
\mathbb{J}_{f}|_{\pi}
admits the Kraus form:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\left[\rho\right]=\sum_{i}K_{i}(\pi)\,\rho\,K_{i}(\pi)^{\dagger}\,,

(163)

where
i
ranges on a possibly uncountable set, and
K_{i}(\pi)
are
\pi
-dependent Kraus operators. Then, thanks to Eq. (
150
), we can express
\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{Tf}|_{\pi}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{Tf}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\left[\rho\right]=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi^{-1}}\left[\rho\right]=\sum_{i}K_{i}(\pi^{-1})\,\rho\,K_{i}(\pi^{-1})^{\dagger}\,,

(164)

proving that
\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{Tf}|_{\pi}
is also CP, and thus
Tf\in\mathcal{M}^{-}
. This shows that the subset
\mathcal{M}^{+}\cup\mathcal{M}^{-}
of
\mathcal{M}
is stable under the transformation
T
. It should be noticed that since this is a strict subset, it was not obvious from the beginning that this would be the case.

We have seen that one can give a necessary condition for
f
to be in
\mathcal{M}^{+}
in terms of the partial order induced by the pointwise order of real functions. It is then a remarkable fact that one can introduce a partial order that implies the pointwise one, and that can be used to completely characterise
\mathcal{M}^{+}
and
\mathcal{M}^{-}
. To this end, we need to define the concept of positive definite continuous functions. These are functions
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that for any vector of reals
\{t_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
of arbitrary size
n
, the matrix
A
defined in coordinates as
A_{i,j}=h(t_{i}-t_{j})
is positive semidefinite. This class of functions is closed under multiplication, and the elements of the set are uniformly bounded by their value in zero. Finally, a key result called Bochner’s theorem says that a function is positive definite if and only if it is the Fourier transform of a finite positive measure on
\mathbb{R}

Reed and Simon 1975
. This last property is the key ingredient of Thm.
14
.

Then, we define the partial order
\preceq
on
\mathcal{M}
by saying that
f_{1}\preceq f_{2}
if
f_{1}(e^{t})/f_{2}(e^{t})
is positive definite or, equivalently, if the matrix with entries:

\displaystyle A_{i,j}:=\frac{f_{1}(\pi_{i}/\pi_{j})}{f_{2}(\pi_{i}/\pi_{j})}

(165)

is positive semidefinite for any non-zero probability vector
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
of any fixed size
n
. Before proving that
\preceq
is an actual order relation, it is useful to point out that
