\displaystyle\delta l^{2}=4\left(\delta q^{2}+q^{2}\delta\theta^{2}\right)\;,\quad\text{with}\quad q\equiv\sqrt{r}\;,\quad\delta\theta^{2}\equiv\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\rho}[\delta\rho]\right]\;.

(293)

We see then that it is possible to separate the contribution due to the normalization coordinate
r\equiv q^{2}
, and the resulting underlying geometry is spherical with radius
2q
. This has a nontrivial consequence. Namely, for all
f
-defined Fisher metrics in Eq. (
21
), solving geodesics on the set of physical states (
r=q^{2}=1
), is equivalent to solving geodesics on the set of positive operators (
r\in(0,\infty)
).
Formally speaking, one defines the geodesic length on the normalised (
\rho
) and unnormalised (
\rho^{\prime}
) set as

\displaystyle(\Delta l_{f})^{2}:=

\displaystyle\min_{\rho^{\prime}(t)|\rho^{\prime}(0)=r_{0}\rho_{0},\rho^{\prime}(1)=r_{1}\rho_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}t\;K_{f,\rho^{\prime}(t)}(\dot{\rho}^{\prime}(t),\dot{\rho}^{\prime}(t))\;,

(294)

\displaystyle(\Delta\theta_{f})^{2}:=

\displaystyle\min_{\rho(t)|\rho(0)=\rho_{0},\rho(1)=\rho_{1}}\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}t\;K_{f,\rho(t)}(\dot{\rho}(t),\dot{\rho}(t))\;.

(295)

The direct consequence of the above discussion resulting in Eq. (
293
) is the following relation, that holds independently from the choice of
f
,

\displaystyle(\Delta l_{f})^{2}=(r_{0}+r_{1})-2\sqrt{r_{0}r_{1}}\cos(\Delta\theta_{f})\;.

(296)

For example, in the case of the Bures metric
V.1
, one has that the angle
\Delta\theta_{\rm Bures}\equiv d_{B}/2
is the Bures distance given in Eq. (
187
) and
\cos(\Delta\theta_{\rm Bures})=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\rho_{1}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\right]
corresponds to the fidelity between initial and final state, leading to the length
D_{B}
in Eq. (
189
).
Similarly, for the Wigner-Yanase metric one has
\Delta\theta_{\rm WY}\equiv d_{\rm WY}/2
(Eq. (
249
)), and therefore
\cos(\Delta\theta_{\rm WY})=\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\sqrt{\rho_{1}}}\right]
, from which it follows immediately that the unnormalised geodesics length coincides with the contrast function in Eq. (
240
), i.e.,
\Delta l_{\rm WY}\equiv\sqrt{H_{\rm WY}}
.

The above equality Eq. (
296
) shows that integrating any
f
-metrics on the set of normalised and unnormalised states is equivalent. This consideration might help in finding geodesics for other Fisher metrics by suitably choosing the problem between Eq. (
294
) and Eq. (
295
) that is easier to solve.

Finally, notice that in the classical, diagonal case
Abiuso et al. 2022
;
Bengtsson and Zyczkowski 2017
,
\Delta\theta
and
\Delta l
are known in the literature respectively as the
Bhattacharyya angle
(or Bhattacharyya distance) and the
Hellinger distance
.

Appendix A
Derivations from Sec.
II

We present here some derivations that were left implicit in Sec.
II
. First, it should be noticed that the canonical expression of matrix convex functions such that
g(1)=0
is given by
Lesniewski and Ruskai 1999
:

\displaystyle g(x)=a\,(x-1)+b\,(x-1)^{2}+c\,\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x}+\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+s}\,,

(297)

where
b
and
c
are positive constants, and
{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)
is a positive measure with finite mass (i.e.,
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)<\infty
). First, it should be noticed that we can ignore the linear term since, as it is discussed in the main text, it does not contribute to the contrast function. Moreover, it is useful to point out that the limit of integration can be made bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+s}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+s}+\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+s}=

(298)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s)\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+s}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{g}(s^{-1})}{s}\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{1+sx}\,,

(299)

where we performed the change of variable
s\rightarrow s^{-1}
in the second integral. Then, redefining the measure, we can include the second and the third term in Eq. (
297
) into the integral. This is accomplished by introducing the measure
{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s)={\rm d}{\mu}_{g}(s)+b\,\delta(s^{-1})/s+c\,\delta(s)
, where
\delta(x)
denote the Dirac delta function. Plugging this in, we obtain the general form of matrix convex functions such that
g(1)=0
:

\displaystyle g(x)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s)\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x+s}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s^{-1})}{s}\;\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{1+sx}\,,

(300)

up to irrelevant linear terms.

Another expression that was not proved in the main text is Eq. (
7
).This follows from the two identities:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})\left[\rho^{1/2}\right]=(\sigma-\rho)\rho^{-1/2}=\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1/2}(\sigma-\rho)\,,

(301)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\sigma}\mathbb{L}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})\left[\rho^{1/2}\right]=\rho^{-1/2}(\sigma-\rho)=\mathbb{L}_{\rho}^{-1/2}(\sigma-\rho)\,,

(302)

which can be verified by explicit computation. Then, one can rewrite the contrast functions in Eq. (
3
) as:

\displaystyle H_{g}(\rho||\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[g\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}\left[\rho\right]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\rho^{1/2}\,g\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}\left[\rho^{1/2}\right]\right]=

(303)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\Big((\mathbb{R}_{\sigma}\mathbb{L}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\Big)}\;{\Big(g\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\Big)}\;\rho^{-1}\right]=

(304)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\,\Big(g\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})^{-2}[(\rho-\sigma)]\Big)\,\rho^{-1}\right]=

(305)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\,\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}h\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right],

(306)

where in the first line we used the fact that since the superoperator
g\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}
acts on
\rho
on the right only through
\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}
one can extract
\rho^{1/2}
from the square parenthesis, and use the cyclicity of the trace to put it in front; in the second line we used both Eq. (
301
) and Eq. (
302
), in the third line the fact that
(\mathbb{R}_{\sigma}\mathbb{L}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})^{\dagger}=(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}-\mathbbm{1})
and finally the definition of the function
h(x):=g(x)/(x-1)^{2}
. This proves Eq. (
7
).

Hence, from the integral expression in Eq. (
300
) it follows that we can rewrite generic contrast functions as:

\displaystyle H_{g}(\rho||\sigma)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s)\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\,\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}+s)^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]+

(307)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s^{-1})}{s}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\,\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}(1+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]\,.

(308)

Let us first focus on the first trace. In particular, it should be noticed that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}+s)^{-1}=((\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}+s)\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}=(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}\,,

(309)

proving that the first integral coincides with the first integral of Eq. (
8
). Doing the same transformation on the second trace we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\,\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1}(1+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\,(\mathbb{R}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]=

(310)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\big((\mathbb{L}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{R}_{\sigma})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\big)\,(\rho-\sigma)\right]\,,

(311)

where in the last step we used the fact that
(\mathbb{R}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{\dagger}=(\mathbb{L}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{R}_{\sigma})
. Hence, putting everything together, and using the cyclicity of the trace in Eq. (
311
), we finally obtain:

\displaystyle H_{g}(\rho||\sigma)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{g}(s^{-1})}{s}\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)(\mathbb{L}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{R}_{\sigma})^{-1}[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]\,.

(312)

proving Eq. (
8
).

Appendix B
Derivation of the flux of Fisher information

We present here the derivation of Thm.
4
. In particular, we want to study the evolution of the Fisher information:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{f,t}:=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho_{t}\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi_{t}}[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]\,,

(313)

where
\pi_{t}:=\Phi_{t}(\pi)
and
\delta\rho_{t}:=\Phi_{t}(\delta\rho)
. Using the integral expression in Eq. (
10
), we can rewrite the Fisher information as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{f,t}:=2\,{\rm Re}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho_{t}\,(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]\,,

(314)

where it is useful to highlight the fact that this quantity is real. This expression is particularly convenient for calculations, due to the simple form that the derivative of
(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}
takes. In fact, this is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}=-(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}(\mathbb{L}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}})(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}\,,

(315)

where
\dot{\pi}_{t}
is simply the derivative of the state. This expression can be proved by noticing that
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}=\mathbb{L}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}}
(and similarly for
\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}}
) and by taking the derivative of:

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{L}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}})(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}+(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}=

(316)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,\mathds{I}=0\,,

(317)

which directly implies Eq. (
315
). Given this technical tool, we can start analysing the evolution of
\mathcal{F}_{f,t}
under the dynamics generated by the Lindbladian:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{t}[\rho]=-i[H_{t},\rho]+\sum_{\alpha}^{d^{2}}\;\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\,\left(A_{\alpha}(t)\,\rho\,A_{\alpha}(t)^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{A_{\alpha}(t)^{\dagger}\,A_{\alpha}(t),\rho\}\right)\,.

(318)

Notice that, since
\mathcal{F}_{f,t}
is invariant under unitary transformations, there is no contribution coming from the commutator in the previous equation. Moreover, since the derivative is linear, it decomposes into a sum of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{f,t}=\sum_{\alpha}\;\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\,\,\mathcal{I}^{f}_{\alpha}(t)\,,

(319)

where each current
\mathcal{I}^{f}_{\alpha}(t)
only contains the corresponding jump operator
A_{\alpha}(t)
, together with its adjoint. For this reason, without loss of generality, we consider here Lindblad operators generated by a single jump operator. In order to shorten the notation we also assume that the jump operator, denoted by
A
, is time independent, again without loss of generality. We start by rewriting the derivative of the Fisher information as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{f,t}=2\,{\rm Re}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\bigg(2\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\dot{\rho}_{t}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]+\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho_{t}\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}\right)[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]\bigg)\,.

(320)

The second term in the integral can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho_{t}\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}\right)[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\rho_{t}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}(\mathbb{L}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\dot{\pi}_{t}})(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]=

(321)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\,\dot{\pi}_{t}\,B_{s}(t)\right]-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\,\dot{\pi}_{t}\right]=

(322)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{L}({\pi_{t}})\,B_{s}(t)\right]-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\,\mathcal{L}({\pi_{t}})\right]\,,

(323)

where we introduced the notation
B_{s}(t):=(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}[\delta\rho_{t}]
. On the other hand, the first term in Eq. (
320
) simply gives:

\displaystyle 2\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\delta\dot{\rho}_{t}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}})^{-1}[\delta\rho_{t}]\right]=

\displaystyle=2\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathcal{L}(\delta\rho_{t})B_{s}(t)\right]=2\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathcal{L}((\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}})[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}])B_{s}(t)\right]=

(324)

\displaystyle=2\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathcal{L}(B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\pi_{t})B_{s}(t)\right]+2s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathcal{L}(\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)^{\dagger})B_{s}(t)\right]\,,

(325)

where in the first line we have multiplied and divided by
(\mathbb{R}_{\pi_{t}}+s\,\mathbb{L}_{\pi_{t}})
to obtain
B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}
. We can now proceed in summing up Eq. (
323
) and Eq. (
325
). Due to the number of terms that will appear, though, we first consider the first traces in both equations, and then the second ones. Hence, summing the first term in Eq. (
323
) and the first of Eq. (
325
), and explicitly expanding the Lindbladian, we obtain:

\displaystyle 2\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\right]-\cancel{\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}A\,B_{s}(t)\right]}-\mathrm{Tr}\left[A^{\dagger}A\,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)\right]+

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A\,\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}\,B_{s}(t)\right]+\cancel{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}A\,B_{s}(t)\right]}+\cancel{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}A\,\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)\right]}\,,

(326)

where the first three terms come from Eq. (
325
), and the last three from Eq. (
323
).
Notice that the second term corresponding to Eq. (
325
) cancels with the last two terms coming from Eq. (
323
), since we can take the Hermitian conjugate of the last trace without affecting the result (thanks to the real part in Eq. (
320
)). The remaining terms can be further simplified to give:

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)A\,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)A^{\dagger}A\,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}\,B_{s}(t)B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A\right]=

(327)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[A^{\dagger},B_{s}(t)]\,AB_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\right]+\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,A^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\,[A,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]\right]=

(328)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,B_{s}(t)A^{\dagger}\,[A,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]\right]+\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,A^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\,[A,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]\right]=

(329)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[A^{\dagger},B_{s}(t)][A,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]\right]=

(330)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[A,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]^{\dagger}[A,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]\right]\,,

(331)

where in the first line we used the cyclicity of the trace to put in evidence
\pi_{t}
, then in the third line we took the complex conjugate of the trace (again exploiting the fact that only the real part contributes to the Fisher information), and used the identity
[X,Y]^{\dagger}=[Y^{\dagger},X^{\dagger}]=-[X^{\dagger},Y^{\dagger}]
.

We can now pass to examine the last two terms in Eq. (
323
) and Eq. (
325
). Since the steps are completely analogous to the ones in the previous derivation, we present them all together. Carrying them out gives:

\displaystyle 2s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\right]-\cancel{s\mathrm{Tr}\left[A^{\dagger}A\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\right]}-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}A\,B_{s}(t)\right]+

(332)

\displaystyle-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)A\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}\right]+\cancel{\frac{s}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)A^{\dagger}A\pi_{t}\right]}+\cancel{\frac{s}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)\pi_{t}A^{\dagger}A\right]}=

(333)

\displaystyle=s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger},A^{\dagger}]\,B_{s}(t)A\right]+s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}\,[B_{s}(t),A]\right]=

(334)

\displaystyle=-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,A^{\dagger}B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}\,[B_{s}(t),A]\right]+s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}\,[B_{s}(t),A]\right]=

(335)

\displaystyle=s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[B_{s}(t)^{\dagger},A^{\dagger}][B_{s}(t),A]\right]=

(336)

\displaystyle=-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[B_{s}(t),A]^{\dagger}[B_{s}(t),A]\right]=

(337)

\displaystyle=-s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[A,B_{s}(t)]^{\dagger}[A,B_{s}(t)]\right]\,.

(338)

This concludes the proof of Thm.
4
. In fact, it is straightforward to generalise to the case of many time dependent jump operators, which gives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{f,t}=\sum_{\alpha}\;\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\,\,\mathcal{I}^{f}_{\alpha}(t)=

(339)

\displaystyle=-2\,\sum_{\alpha}\;\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\,\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\bigg(\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[A_{\alpha}(t),B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]^{\dagger}[A_{\alpha}(t),B_{s}(t)^{\dagger}]\right]+s\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\pi_{t}\,[A_{\alpha}(t),B_{s}(t)]^{\dagger}[A_{\alpha}(t),B_{s}(t)]\right]\bigg)\,,

(340)

where we can drop the real part, as both terms in the integral are positive definite.

Appendix C
Derivations from Sec.
III.3

In this appendix we first prove Thm.
13
, and then proceed to derive the structural characterisation of Fisher detailed balanced Lindbladians presented in Eq. (
138
).

Due to the amount of different notions of adjoints used in the following, we remind the reader about the notation. There are three different scalar product used, namely the Hilbert-Schmidt one,
K_{\pi}^{o}
and
K_{f,\pi}
, which induce the following adjoints:

Hilbert

\displaystyle\text{-Schmidt:}\qquad\;\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[AX(B)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{\dagger}(A)B\right]\,;

(341)

\displaystyle K_{\pi}^{o}:\qquad\qquad\;\;\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathcal{J}_{\pi}[OB]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\widetilde{O}^{o}(A)\mathcal{J}_{\pi}[B]\right]\,;

(342)

\displaystyle K_{f,\pi}:\qquad\qquad\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[\mathcal{O}B]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{f}A)\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[B]\right]\,,

(343)

where
O
and
\mathcal{O}
are a superoperator on the space of observables or on the state space, respectively, while
X
is a generic bounded operator. We also remind the reader that we use the notation
\mathcal{J}_{\pi}:=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}
. Then, the adjoint with respect to
K_{\pi}^{o}
or
K_{f,\pi}
are related to the Hilbert-Schmidt one by the relation:

\displaystyle\widetilde{O}^{o}=\mathcal{J}_{\pi}^{-1}\circ O^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{J}_{\pi}\,;\qquad\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{f}=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\circ\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\circ\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}\,,

(344)

as it can be verified directly from the definition. In this context, self-adjointness with respect to
K^{o}_{\pi}
is equivalent to the condition
\mathcal{J}_{\pi}\circ O=O^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{J}_{\pi}
, while for the Fisher scalar product it can be expressed by the equality
\mathcal{O}\circ\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\circ\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}
. Thanks to this characterisation we can prove the following useful result:

Lemma 2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{O}
and
O
are adjoint of each other,
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\equiv O
. Then, if
[\mathcal{O},\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}]=0
, the two conditions of self-adjointness and skew-self-adjointness with respect to
K_{f,\pi}
and
K_{\pi}^{o}
coincide.

Proof.

First of all, it should be noticed that the adjoint of the modular operator takes the form
(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})^{\dagger}=\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}=(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})^{-1}
, while for the right multiplication operator it holds that
(\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{\dagger}=\mathbb{L}_{\pi}
. Using these two properties we can show that the (skew-)self-adjointness with respect to
K_{f,\pi}
is equivalent to the corresponding notion for
K_{\pi}^{o}
. In fact, the following relations are equivalent:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{f}=\pm\mathcal{O}\iff(\mathcal{O}\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi})^{\dagger}=\pm\,\mathcal{O}\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\;\iff\;f((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})^{-1})\mathbb{L}_{\pi}O=\pm\,\mathcal{O}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}f(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})\;\iff

(345)

\displaystyle\iff\;\cancel{f(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}O=\pm\,\cancel{f(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})}O^{\dagger}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}\iff\;\widetilde{O}^{o}=\pm O\,,

(346)

where in the first line we used the definition
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\equiv O
, and in the second line we exploited the property
f(x)=xf(x^{-1})
, together with the commutation between
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
to push
f(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})
to the left of both equations.
∎

This lemma is particularly useful because it allows to reduce the question about the equivalence of Def.
1
and Def.
3
to the decision about the commutation of the Lindbladian with the modular operator
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
.

C.1
Proof of Theorem
13

The aim of this section is to prove Thm.
13
, which we repeat here for convenience:

Theorem
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

the generator of the dynamics in the Heisenberg picture
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
satisfies the adjointness relations in Def.
1
;

2.

the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the structural characterisation in Def.
2
.

These conditions imply the condition:

3.

the generator of the dynamics in the Schroedinger picture
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the adjointness relations in Def.
3
.

Moreover, if the Hamiltonian
H
is non-degenerate the three conditions are equivalent.

First, it should be noticed that the equivalence between condition
1
and
2
was already proved by Alicki in
Alicki 1976
, so we postpone the proof to App.
C.3
where we characterise the Lindbladians satisfying condition
3
. Then, if
H
is non-degenerate, this provides a proof of the structural definition of condition
2
.

Proof.

First it should be noticed that if condition
2
is satisfied, the Lindbladian commutes with the modular operator
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
. In fact, starting from the characterisation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\rho)=-i[H,\rho]+\sum_{\omega,i}\;\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\left(A_{i}^{\omega}\,\rho\,(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}A_{i}^{\omega},\rho\}\right)\,,

(347)

it is a matter of straightforward calculations to verify that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}(\rho))

\displaystyle=-i\pi[H,\rho]\pi^{-1}+\sum_{\omega,i}\;\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\left((\pi\,A_{i}^{\omega}\pi^{-1})\,\pi\,\rho\,\pi^{-1}(\pi^{-1}A_{i}^{\omega}\,\pi)^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\pi\{(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}A_{i}^{\omega},\rho\}\pi^{-1}\right)=

(348)

\displaystyle=-i[H,\pi\rho\pi^{-1}]+\sum_{\omega,i}\;\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\cancel{e^{\omega}}\cancel{e^{-\omega}}\left(A_{i}^{\omega}\,\pi\,\rho\,\pi^{-1}(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}A_{i}^{\omega},\pi\,\rho\pi^{-1}\}\right)=

(349)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}(\rho))

(350)

where we used the condition
[H,\pi]=0
, together with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}(A_{i}^{\omega})=e^{\omega}\,A_{i}^{\omega}
and
(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}=A_{i}^{-\omega}
. Since condition
2
is equivalent to condition
1
, and
[\mathcal{L},\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}]=0
, thanks to Lemma
2
this means that
\mathcal{L}
has the same self-adjointness properties with respect to
K_{\pi}^{o}
and
K_{f,\pi}
. This proves the forward implication.

Let us prove the reverse, namely that condition
3
is equivalent to condition
1
for non-degenerate Hamiltonians. Let us first focus on the unitary part. Then, using
\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}=-\mathcal{U}
we can rewrite the skew-adjointness condition as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\circ\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}=-\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\circ\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\qquad\iff\qquad\mathcal{U}\circ\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\circ\mathcal{U}\,.

(351)

Then, applying the two operators in the last equation to the identity, we can verify that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\circ\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}(\mathbbm{1})=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\circ\mathcal{U}(\mathbbm{1})

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{U}(\pi)=-i\,\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}([H,\mathbbm{1}])\implies[H,\pi]=0\,.

(352)

Notice that this result is generic, i.e., no assumptions on the spectrum of
H
need to be made. This directly implies the commutation
[\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}]=0
, so again thanks to Lemma
2
we have that for the unitary part condition
3
and
1
are always equivalent.

Let us now focus on Eq. (
352
). Since
H
and
\pi
commute, we can find a common set of eigenvectors, which we denote by
\{\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\}
. Moreover, the basis
\{\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|\}
gives a set of common eigenvectors to
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, as it can be verified by direct calculation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|)=-i(H_{\alpha}-H_{\beta})\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|\,;\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}(\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|)=\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|\,.

(353)

Both superoperators have constant eigenvalues for all eigenvectors of the form
\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\alpha}\right.\right|
. Under the assumption of continuity under small perturbations, we can also assume that each eigenvector such that
\alpha\neq\beta
has a different eigenvalue (non-degenerate gap condition). These two observations together then imply that any superoperator commuting with
\mathcal{U}
needs to commute with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
as well. In the main text we showed how normality of
\mathcal{L}
implies
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}]=0
. Then, thanks
to the considerations above, we also have that:

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}]=0\ .

(354)

Thus, thanks once again to Lemma
2
, we have that
(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}})_{f}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
implies
(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}})^{o}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

It should be noticed that the non-degeneracy of the spectrum is needed to prove the commutation relation in Eq. (
354
). The same equivalence can be proved if
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
has some functional dependence on
\mathcal{U}
. Take for example the thermal scenario, i.e.,
\pi\propto\exp[-\beta H]
. Then the modular operator takes the form
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}=\exp[-i\beta\mathcal{U}]
. Due to normality of the Lindbladian (which implies
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}]=0
), we directly obtain Eq. (
354
), proving the equivalence without further assumptions on the spectrum of
\pi
or
H
.

C.2
Def.
3
is weaker in general

Whereas the constraints coming from Def.
1
imply the ones in Def.
3
, the reverse does not hold in general. In fact, this is connected with the commutation between
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
. Whereas in the first definition of detailed balance these two operators always commute, this is not the case for the Fisher detailed balance dissipators. This leads to a less constrained evolution of the coherences, as it will be shown in the following.

First, as it was discussed in the proof in the previous section, if
\{\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\}
is an eigenbasis for
\pi
, then
\{\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|\}
are eigenvectors for
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, with eigenvalues:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}(\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|)=\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|\,.

(355)

Since the steady state
\pi
is always assumed to be full rank, proving that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
commutes with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
is equivalent to requiring that the matrix elements

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\delta|\beta}^{\gamma|\alpha}\equiv\left.\left\langle{\gamma}\right.\right|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}(\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|)\left.\left|{\delta}\right.\right\rangle

(356)

satisfy the following condition

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}},\Phi_{\pi}]=0\quad\iff\quad(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\delta|\beta}^{\gamma|\alpha}\left(\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}-\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}\right)=0\,.

(357)

Equivalently, this means that matrix elements of
\mathcal{D}
can be nonzero only if:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\delta|\beta}^{\gamma|\alpha}\neq 0\quad\implies\quad\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}\,.

(358)

In the following we show that a slightly more general condition follows from the requirement that
(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}})_{f}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
for all standard monotone functions
f
. Indeed, this condition can be written in coordinates as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\circ\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}\quad\iff\quad(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\,f\left(\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}\right)\pi_{\beta}=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\alpha|\gamma}^{\beta|\delta}\,f\left(\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}\right)\pi_{\delta}\,.

(359)

At this point it is useful to introduce the notation
e^{-\omega_{1}}:=\pi_{\alpha}/\pi_{\beta}
and
e^{-\omega_{2}}:=\pi_{\gamma}/\pi_{\delta}
.
Grouping the functional dependence on one side of the equation we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\;\pi_{\beta}}{(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\alpha|\gamma}^{\beta|\delta}\;\pi_{\delta}}=\frac{f\left(e^{-\omega_{2}}\right)}{f\left(e^{-\omega_{1}}\right)}\,.

(360)

It should be noticed that the left hand side of the equation does not depend on the function
f
, so the coordinates of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
are zero unless
\omega_{1}=\pm\omega_{2}
(notice that one cannot rule out the case
\omega_{1}=-\omega_{2}
, since this follows from the symmetry of standard monotone functions
f(x)=x\,f(x^{-1})
).

Then, the only non-zero elements of a Fisher self-adjoint
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
are the ones for which either of the conditions:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\neq 0\implies\left(\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}\right)\lor\left(\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\delta}}{\pi_{\gamma}}\right)\,,

(361)

are satisfied.
Comparing this result with Eq. (
358
) directly shows that in general
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
does not commute with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, so Def.
3
is weaker than Def.
1
(at the end of the section we present an explicit example showing this). For this reason, it is interesting to explore which constraints Eq. (
361
) imposes on the Lindbladian. First, it should be noticed that since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
is adjoint preserving, its coordinates satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\dagger})=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}(A)^{\dagger}\quad\iff\quad(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}=\overline{(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\gamma|\alpha}^{\delta|\beta}}\,.

(362)

Then, combining Eq. (
360
), Eq. (
361
), and Eq. (
362
), we can see that:

•

populations and coherences do not mix, and the populations on the diagonal satisfy the classical detailed balance condition. In fact, from Eq. (
361
) we see that from
\alpha=\beta
it follows that
\gamma=\delta
(assuming that
\pi
is non-degenerate), and the transition probabilities are related by the standard detailed balance condition:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\gamma|\alpha}\,\pi_{\alpha}=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\alpha|\gamma}_{\alpha|\gamma}\,\pi_{\gamma}\,.

(363)

Moreover, the dynamics of the coherences can be split in two cases:

•

the one for which
\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}
, implying the following relation:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\,\pi_{\beta}=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\beta|\delta}_{\alpha|\gamma}\,\pi_{\delta}=\overline{(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\beta|\delta}^{\alpha|\gamma}}\,\pi_{\delta}\,.

(364)

This property is satisfied also in the Alicki’s definition of detailed balanced generator.

•

the additional transitions between coherences, corresponding to the case
\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\delta}}{\pi_{\gamma}}
.
In this case the rate are given by:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\,\pi_{\beta}=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\beta|\delta}_{\alpha|\gamma}\,\pi_{\gamma}=\overline{(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\beta|\delta}^{\alpha|\gamma}}\,\pi_{\gamma}\,.

(365)

These rates are the only novelty compared with the ones coming from Def.
1
, and are the ones that prevent
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
from commuting with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
.

In order to justify the preference for Def.
3
as the quantum generalisation of detailed balance, we argue here that this last case is still physically sensible. Consider indeed two coherences terms
\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|
and
\left.\left|{\gamma}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\delta}\right.\right|
such that
\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}
. Then, from Eq. (
364
) it follows that the ratio between the currents induced between the two coherences is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{\big|(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\big|}{\big|{(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\beta|\delta}^{\alpha|\gamma}}\big|}=\frac{\pi_{\delta}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\alpha}}\,.

(366)

The additional freedom given by Eq. (
365
) corresponds to the possibility of the matrix element
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\delta|\alpha}_{\gamma|\beta}
to be non-zero. It should be noticed, though, that the current between the two coherences is consistent with Eq. (
366
):

\displaystyle\frac{\big|(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\delta|\alpha}_{\gamma|\beta}\big|}{\big|({\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\alpha|\delta}_{\beta|\gamma}\big|}=\frac{\pi_{\delta}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\alpha}}\,.

(367)

Thus, the difference between the detailed balance condition in Def.
1
and the Fisher one (i.e., Def.
3
), is that the first allows the coherences
\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|
and
\left.\left|{\gamma}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\delta}\right.\right|
to communicate but prohibits interactions between
\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|
and
\left.\left|{\delta}\right.\right\rangle\left.\left\langle{\gamma}\right.\right|
, while the latter allows the dynamics to connect both off-diagonal elements, while still keeping the ratio between the two currents detailed balanced. Since there is no clear argument to disregard this second set of transitions, it seems that Def.
3
should be preferred.

Finally, before moving on to the characterisation of the Lindbladian operators satisfying Def.
3
we present here an example of
\mathcal{L}
which is detailed balance in the Fisher sense, but not according to Def.
1
. Consider a two-levels system equilibrating to the state

\displaystyle\pi_{\beta}=\frac{\left.\left|{0}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{0}\right.\right|+e^{-\beta}\left.\left|{1}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{1}\right.\right|}{1+e^{-\beta}}\,.

(368)

We consider the Hamiltonian of the system to be completely degenerate, i.e.,
H\propto\mathbbm{1}
, which implies
\mathcal{U}=0
. Then, it follows from an explicit calculation that the Lindbladian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\rho)=A\rho A^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\rho,A^{\dagger}A\}\,,

(369)

with the jump operator given by
A=\left.\left|{0}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{1}\right.\right|+\sqrt{e^{-\beta}}\left.\left|{1}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{0}\right.\right|
satisfies
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{f}=\mathcal{L}
, but not
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{o}=\mathcal{L}
.

C.3
Structural characterisation of Def.
3

This section is devoted to the derivation of the structural form of Lindbladians satisfying the detailed balance condition in Def.
3
. Restricting the derivation to the case in which
\mathcal{L}
commutes with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}^{-1}_{\pi}
also proves the equivalence between
Def.
1
and Def.
2
.

With the hindsight of the previous section it is useful to expand the Lindbladian operator in terms of the following eigenbasis:

\displaystyle F^{\omega}_{\alpha}:=\left\{\left.\left|{\gamma}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\alpha}\right.\right|\bigg|\;\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\alpha}}=e^{\omega}\right\}\,.

(370)

It is also useful to introduce the function
\beta_{\alpha}(\omega):=\left\{\beta|\,\pi_{\beta}=\pi_{\alpha}e^{\omega}\right\}
, namely a function that returns the index
\beta
such that
\frac{\pi_{\beta}}{\pi_{\alpha}}=e^{\omega}
. In order to keep the notation clear we also define
\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)
and
\delta_{\alpha}(\omega)
exactly in the same way. The elements of the eigenbasis in Eq. (
370
) have the property that:

\displaystyle(F^{\omega}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}=F^{-\omega}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}\,;\qquad\qquad\pi F^{\omega}_{\alpha}=e^{\omega}\,F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\pi\,.

(371)

Generically, one can express the action of the dissipator as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}(\rho):

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta,\\
\gamma,\delta\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma|\alpha}_{\delta|\beta}\;\left.\left|{\gamma}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\alpha}\right.\right|\,\rho\,(\left.\left|{\delta}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\beta}\right.\right|)^{\dagger}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega+\omega_{2})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger}\,,

(372)

where we implicitly defined
\pi_{\gamma}/\pi_{\alpha}=:e^{\omega}
,
\pi_{\beta}/\pi_{\alpha}=:e^{\omega_{1}}
and
\pi_{\delta}/\pi_{\gamma}=:e^{\omega_{2}}
. This expression is particularly useful because it allows for a straightforward application of the constraints in Eq. (
361
). In fact, we have that:

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\gamma}}{\pi_{\delta}}\qquad

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}\,;

(373)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{\alpha}}{\pi_{\beta}}=\frac{\pi_{\delta}}{\pi_{\gamma}}\qquad

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\omega_{1}=-\omega_{2}\,.

(374)

Hence, the sum above can be restricted to the case
\omega_{1}=\pm\omega_{2}
, giving:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega\\
\omega_{1}\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega+\omega_{1})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega-\omega_{1})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega-2\omega_{1}}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger}\,.

(375)

It should be noticed that the case
\omega_{1}=0
is included in the first sum, so that we have to impose the constraint
\omega_{1}\neq 0
in the second sum.
In this way
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
naturally splits in two parts,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
(i.e., the operator in the first sum) corresponding to the component of the dissipator commuting with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
. Interestingly, the condition
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}^{o}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
implies
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\equiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
, so characterising the latter provides the structural form of Lindbladians detailed balance according to Def.
1
.

Whereas
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
directly commutes with
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, we can apply a transformation to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
to make it commuting. In particular, it should be noticed that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
transforms under the transposition superoperator
\Theta
as:

\displaystyle[\Theta\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}](\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega-\omega_{1})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;\left(F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega-2\omega_{1}}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger}\right)^{T}=

(376)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega-\omega_{1})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;\left.\left|{\pi_{\alpha}e^{\omega-\omega_{1}}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\pi_{\alpha}}\right.\right|\rho\left.\left|{\pi_{\alpha}e^{\omega_{1}}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\pi_{\alpha}e^{\omega}}\right.\right|=

(377)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega-\omega_{1})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;F^{\omega-\omega_{1}}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega-\omega_{1}}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega+\omega_{1})|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega)|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger}\,,

(378)

where in the second line we used the abuse of notation
\left.\left|{\pi_{\alpha}}\right.\right\rangle
for
\left.\left|{\alpha}\right.\right\rangle
, and in the last line we made an implicit change of variables. From Eq. (
378
) we can see that
\Theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
takes a form completely analogous to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
. For this reason, it directly follows that:

\displaystyle[\Theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}},\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}]=0\,.

(379)

This condition allows to lift the characterisation for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
, as it will be shown in the following.

We begin by studying
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
. Notice that the coordinates of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
are related by:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega+\omega_{1})|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}=e^{\omega}\,(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})_{\alpha|\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}^{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})|\delta_{\alpha}(\omega+\omega_{1})}\,,

(380)

as it can be verified from Eq. (
360
). Then, we can proceed with the standard approach to diagonalise
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}

Alicki 1976
;
Carlen and Maas 2017
. To this end, it is useful to introduce a new basis of operators, given by
\left\{X^{\omega}_{m}\right\}=\left\{\Delta_{i}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq d}\cup\left\{F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\omega\neq 0}
, where
\left\{\Delta_{i}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
is an orthonormal basis for the diagonal matrices, and
\Delta_{1}=\mathbbm{1}/\sqrt{d}
. Then, we can rewrite
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
in this basis as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(0)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\beta}(0)|\beta}\;F^{0}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{0}_{\beta})^{\dagger}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta\\
\omega\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega)|\beta}\;F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega}_{\beta})^{\dagger}=

(381)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\end{subarray}}\;D_{i,j}^{0}\;\Delta_{i}\,\rho\,(\Delta_{j})^{\dagger}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta,\\
\omega\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}\;F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega}_{\beta})^{\dagger}\,,

(382)

where we introduced the coefficients
D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}:=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega)|\beta}
and

\displaystyle D^{0}_{i,j}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\alpha|\alpha}_{\beta|\beta}\,\bar{U}_{\alpha,i}\,U_{j,\beta}\,,

(383)

where
U
is the unitary defined by
\Delta_{i}:=\sum_{\alpha}U_{i,\alpha}F_{\alpha}
. In order to make the dissipator explicitly trace preserving, we highlight the terms containing the identity operator. This leads to the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(\rho)=\left\{K,\rho\right\}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\neq(1,1)\\
\omega\end{subarray}}D^{\omega}_{i,j}\;X^{\omega}_{i}\,\rho\,(X_{j}^{\omega})^{\dagger}\,,

(384)

where
K:=\frac{D^{0}_{1,1}}{2d}\mathbbm{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{i}D^{0}_{i,1}\Delta_{i}
, and there is no additional term thanks to the reality of
D^{0}_{i,j}
. It should be noticed that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
only gives contributions out of the diagonal, so one can impose trace preservation on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
alone, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1})=0
. This directly implies that
K=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\neq(1,1)\end{subarray}}D^{\omega}_{i,j}(X_{j}^{\omega})^{\dagger}X_{i}^{\omega}\right)
, so the dissipator can be rewritten in Lindblad form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(\rho)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\neq(1,1)\\
\omega\end{subarray}}D^{\omega}_{i,j}\;\left(X_{i}^{\omega}\,\rho\,(X_{j}^{\omega})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{(X_{j}^{\omega})^{\dagger}X_{i}^{\omega},\rho\right\}\right)\,.

(385)

The property of the dissipator of being adjoint preserving implies that the matrix
D^{\omega}_{i,j}
is Hermitian. Then, there exists a unitary matrix
V
, such that
D^{\omega}_{i,j}=\sum_{m,n}V^{\omega}_{i,m}(\lambda^{\omega}_{m}\delta^{m}_{n})(V^{\omega})_{n,j}^{\dagger}
. We can then define the jump operators as
A_{m}^{\omega}:=\sum_{i}X^{\omega}_{i}V^{\omega}_{i,m}
. This allows us to recast Eq. (
385
) in the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\neq(1,1)\\
\omega\end{subarray}}\;\lambda^{\omega}_{m}\;V^{\omega}_{i,m}(V^{\omega})_{m,j}^{\dagger}\left(X^{\omega}_{i}\,\rho\,(X^{\omega}_{j})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{(X^{\omega}_{j})^{\dagger}X^{\omega}_{i},\rho\right\}\right)=

(386)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,\omega}\;\lambda^{\omega}_{m}\;\left(A^{\omega}_{m}\,\rho\,(A^{\omega}_{m})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{(A^{\omega}_{m})^{\dagger}A^{\omega}_{m},\rho\right\}\right)\,.

(387)

We can now characterise the properties of the jump operators and of the rates in the previous equation. First, it should be noticed that since
X_{i}^{\omega}
are eigenoperators of the modular operator
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
, the same holds for
A_{m}^{\omega}
, as the unitary does not mix
X_{i}^{\omega}
s with different
\omega
s. Moreover, Eq. (
380
) implies that
D^{\omega}_{\alpha,\beta}=e^{\omega}\,D^{-\omega}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega),\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}
, where we used the same indices as in the equation. This relation shows that
D^{-\omega}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega),\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}
can be diagonalised as
e^{-\omega}\lambda^{\omega}_{m}\delta_{n}^{m}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}(V^{\omega})_{m,\alpha}^{\dagger}D^{-\omega}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega),\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}(V^{\omega})_{\beta,n}
. This allows to deduce the following two facts: first, the spectrum of
D^{\omega}_{\alpha,\beta}
satisfies
\lambda_{i}^{\omega}=e^{\omega}\,\lambda_{i}^{-\omega}
; second, since
(X^{\omega}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}=X^{-\omega}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}
, it also holds that:

\displaystyle(A^{\omega}_{i})^{\dagger}=\sum_{\alpha}\;X^{-\omega}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}(V^{\omega}_{\alpha,i})^{\dagger}=\sum_{\alpha}\;X^{-\omega}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}V^{-\omega}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega),i}=A^{-\omega}_{i}\,.

(388)

Thus, the dissipator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
satisfies the same conditions of Def.
2
, namely:

1.

(A_{i}^{\omega})^{\dagger}=A_{i}^{-\omega}
;

2.

\pi\,A_{i}^{\omega}\,\pi^{-1}=e^{\omega}\,A_{i}^{\omega}
;

3.

\lambda_{i}^{\omega}=e^{\omega}\,\lambda_{i}^{-\omega}
.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
is the only component of the dissipator if one uses Def.
1
, this proves the equivalence between this notion of detailed balance and the structural characterisation in Def.
2
.

We can now pass to characterise
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
. Thanks to Eq. (
378
) we can rewrite it as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\omega,\\
\omega_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega+\omega_{1})|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega)|\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})}\;(F^{\omega}_{\alpha}\,\rho\,(F^{\omega}_{\beta_{\alpha}(\omega_{1})})^{\dagger})^{T}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\neq\beta,\\
\omega\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\alpha}(\omega)|\beta}\;F^{\omega}_{\beta}\,\rho^{T}\,(F^{\omega}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}=

(389)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\neq\beta,\\
\omega\end{subarray}}\;(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\delta_{\beta}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\beta}\;F^{\omega}_{\beta}\,\rho^{T}\,(F^{\omega}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,,

(390)

where we eliminated the dependence on
\omega_{1}
by enforcing the constraint
\alpha\neq\beta
. Finally, in the last line we exchanged the dummy indexes
\gamma
and
\delta
to highlight the analogy with the other part of the Lindbladian. Indeed, define the matrix
\widetilde{D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}}:=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\delta_{\beta}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\beta}
for any
\alpha\neq\beta
and zero on the diagonal. It is interesting to compare it to the off-diagonal elements of
D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}=(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)|\alpha}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega)|\beta}
: as it can be seen, the two are related by an exchange
\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)\leftrightarrow\delta_{\beta}(\omega)
. Moreover, thanks to Eq. (
367
) it also holds that:

\displaystyle\widetilde{D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}}=e^{\omega}\,\widetilde{D}_{\delta_{\beta}(\omega),\gamma_{\alpha}(\omega)}^{-\omega}\,,

(391)

which shows the analogy with
D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}
even further. Using Eq. (
362
) it also follows that
\widetilde{D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}}
is Hermitian, so there exists a unitary matrix
W
such that
\widetilde{D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}}=\sum_{m,n}W^{\omega}_{\alpha,m}(\mu^{\omega}_{m}\delta^{m}_{n})(W^{\omega})_{n,\beta}^{\dagger}
. We can then define the jump operators
B_{m}^{\omega}:=\sum_{\alpha}(W^{\omega}_{m,\alpha})^{\dagger}F^{\omega}_{\alpha}
. This allows to rewrite
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\neq\beta,\\
\omega\end{subarray}}\;W^{\omega}_{\alpha,m}(\mu^{\omega}_{m}\delta^{m}_{n})(W^{\omega})_{n,\beta}^{\dagger}\;F^{\omega}_{\beta}\,\rho^{T}\,(F^{\omega}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}=\sum_{m}\;\mu^{\omega}_{m}\,B_{m}^{\omega}\,\rho^{T}(B_{m}^{\omega})^{\dagger}\,.

(392)

In analogy with the previous case it also holds that
\mu^{\omega}_{i}=e^{\omega}\mu^{-\omega}_{i}
and
(B_{m}^{\omega})^{\dagger}=B_{m}^{-\omega}
, together with the fact that
B_{m}^{\omega}
is an eigenoperator of
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}
. Still, there is one crucial difference with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
: since all the diagonal elements of
\widetilde{D_{\alpha,\beta}^{\omega}}
are zero, this matrix is traceless, meaning that the sum of the eigenvalues is also zero. Hence, whereas
\lambda_{i}^{\omega}\geq 0
for all
i
and
\omega
, we have the extra constraint
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}^{\omega}=0
, implying the negativity of some of the
\mu_{i}^{\omega}
. Putting everything together, we finally obtain the characterisation in Eq. (
138
).

Appendix D
Derivations from Sec.
IV.2

We report here the explicit computation that were omitted in Sec.
IV.2
. We start by discussing how the defining properties of the standard monotone functions are reflected on the Fisher information operators. First, the property that
f(1)=1
implies that
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
reduces to the multiplication by
\pi
for commuting operators, as it was shown in Eq. (
19
). We repeat the derivation here for completeness: it should be noticed that the modular operator acts trivially on the commutant of
\pi
, that is
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}[A]=A
for
[\pi,A]=0
. For this reason
f(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})[A]=f(\mathds{I})[A]=A
, where in the last step we used the fact that
f(1)=1
. Hence, if
[\pi,A]=0
, then
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[A]=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}[A]=A\pi
.

We can now pass to the second condition that standard monotone functions should satisfy, namely
{f(x)=xf(x^{-1})}
. This enforces
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
to be adjoint preserving. Indeed, one has that, if
A=A^{\dagger}
, it follows that:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[A])^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}f\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}[A])^{\dagger}=\mathbb{L}_{\pi}f\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}[A^{\dagger}]=\cancel{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}}\,\cancel{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}f\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}[A]=\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}[A]\,,

(393)

where we used the fact that
(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}[A])^{\dagger}=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}[A^{\dagger}]
(and similarly for
\mathbb{R}_{\pi}
), the commutation
[\mathbb{L}_{\pi},\mathbb{R}_{\pi}]=0
and the property
{f(x)=xf(x^{-1})}
.

Finally, from the monotonicity of
f
it follows that (see Thm.
2
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi(A)\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}[\Phi(A)]\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[A\,\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]\right]\,,

(394)

for every self-adjoint operator
A
and CPTP map
\Phi
. By polarisation this means that
\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\right)\Phi\leq\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
, which is the first of the two conditions in Eq.
145
. By multiplying the two sides of the inequality by
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\pi}
on the right and on the left we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\pi}\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\right)\Phi\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\pi}=\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\pi}\Phi^{\dagger}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1/2}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\right)\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\pi}\Phi^{\dagger}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1/2}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\right)^{\dagger}\leq\mathds{I}\,.

(395)

This inequality is of the form
AA^{\dagger}\leq\mathds{I}
, for
A=\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\pi}\Phi^{\dagger}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1/2}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}
. But then it follows that also
A^{\dagger}A\leq\mathds{I}
, which expands to:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1/2}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\Phi\,\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\Phi^{\dagger}\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1/2}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}\leq\mathds{I}\,.

(396)

Multiplying both sides on the left and on the right by
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{1/2}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}
we finally obtain
\Phi\left(\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}\right)\Phi^{\dagger}\;\leq\;{\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\Phi(\pi)}}
, which proves the second condition in Eq.
145
.

After having solved the question about the properties of
\mathbb{J}_{f}\big|_{\pi}
we present here the explicit derivation of Eq. (
152
) and Eq. (
153
). First it should be noticed that we can rewrite
\mathbb{J}_{f_{\lambda}}\big|_{\pi}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f_{\lambda}}\big|_{\pi}[A]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{R}_{\pi}\,f_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}\boldsymbol{)}[A]=\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}{(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}+\lambda)}+\frac{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}{(1+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})}\right)[A\pi]=

(397)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\cancel{\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}}{\cancel{\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi})}+\frac{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}\cancel{\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}}{\cancel{\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi})}\right)[A\pi]=

(398)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi})^{-1}\right)[\pi A\pi]\,,

(399)

where we used the commutation between
\mathbb{L}_{\pi}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\pi}
to simplify the manipulations. Integrating over
\lambda
then gives Eq. (
152
). A similar manipulation can be carried out for
\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}
, which gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{f}^{-1}\big|_{\pi}[A]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{J}_{Tf}\big|_{\pi^{-1}}[A]=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1})^{-1}\right)[\pi^{-1}A\pi^{-1}]=

(400)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}{(\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1})}+\frac{\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1}\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}}{(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}^{-1}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi}^{-1})}\right)[A]=

(401)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{f}(\lambda)\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{R}_{\pi})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathbb{L}_{\pi})^{-1}\right)[A]\,,

(402)

which proves Eq. (
153
).

Appendix E
Ordering of symmetrised contrast functions

In this section we prove that symmetrised contrast functions satisfy the ordering:

\displaystyle H^{\rm symm}_{Lf_{B}}(\rho||\sigma)\leq H_{g}^{\rm symm}(\rho||\sigma)\leq H_{Lf_{H}}^{\rm symm}(\rho||\sigma)\,.

(403)

The two main ingredients we use are the integral expression in Eq. (
9
), namely:

\displaystyle H^{\rm symm}_{g}(\rho||\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}+(\mathbb{L}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{R}_{\sigma})^{-1}\right)[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]

(404)

and the following Lemma:

Lemma 3
.

For any two full-rank states
\rho
and
\sigma
and any
s\in[0,1]
the following chain of operator inequalities hold:

\displaystyle 2\,(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}\leq\left(\frac{1+s}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\,(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{-1}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1})\,.

(405)

Proof.

The three operators in Eq. (
405
) are all diagonal in the basis given by
\{\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|\}
, as it can be readily verified from the equalities:

\displaystyle 2\,(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}[\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sigma_{j}+\rho_{i}}\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|\,;

(406)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1+s}{2}\right)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{-1}\right)[\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+s}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}+s\,\rho_{i}}+\frac{1}{s\,\sigma_{j}+\rho_{i}}\right)\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|\,;

(407)

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2}}\,(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{-1}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{-1})[\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|]

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{i}}\right)\left.\left|{\sigma_{j}}\right.\right\rangle\!\!\left.\left\langle{\rho_{i}}\right.\right|\,.

(408)

Then the claim follows from the chain of inequalities:

\displaystyle\frac{2}{1+x}\leq\left(\frac{1+s}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1+s\,x}+\frac{1}{s+x}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2x}\,,

(409)

which holds for any positive
x
and
s\in[0,1]
. Moreover, the function in the middle is monotonically decreasing in
s
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the claim. Indeed, starting from Eq. (
404
), and using Eq. (
156
) we have:

\displaystyle H^{\rm symm}_{g}(\rho||\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}N_{g}(s)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}+(\mathbb{L}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{R}_{\sigma})^{-1}\right)[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]=

(410)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\mu_{f}(s)\;\left(\frac{1+s}{2}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\rho-\sigma)\left((\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+s\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}+(\mathbb{R}_{\rho}+s\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{-1}\right)[(\rho-\sigma)]\right]\,,

(411)

where in the second line we took the adjoint of the second term in the trace. Since
{\rm d}\mu_{f}(s)
is a probability distribution and thanks to Lemma
3
, any contrast function is smaller than the one corresponding to
\delta\mu_{f_{H}}(s)=\delta(s)
and larger than the one corresponding to
\delta\mu_{f_{B}}(s)=\delta(s-1)
. Hence, for any symmetrised contrast function Eq. (
403
) holds.

Appendix F
Integral expression of
(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}

Consider the equation:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})[A]=\sigma A+A\rho=X\,.

(412)

One can implicitly solve for
A
as
A=(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})^{-1}[X]
. In this section, we prove that this solution can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle A=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\;e^{-t\sigma}\,X\,e^{-t\rho}\,.

(413)

If this were the case, it follows from Eq. (
412
) that multiplying the right hand side of the equation by

(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})
would give back
X
. Indeed, this can be straightforwardly verified as:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{R}_{\rho})\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\;e^{-t\sigma}\,X\,e^{-t\rho}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\;\left(\sigma\,e^{-t\sigma}\,X\,e^{-t\rho}+e^{-t\sigma}\,X\,e^{-t\rho}\,\rho\right)=

(414)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\;\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t\,}\,e^{-t\sigma}\,X\,e^{-t\rho}\right)=\left(e^{-t\sigma}\,X\,e^{-t\rho}\right)\bigg|_{t=\infty}^{t=0}=X\,,

(415)

which proves the claim.
